THE 1983 ANNUAL TOWN REPORT IS DEDICATED TO
BERTHA SUSAN BROWN
"Lunenburg, with its high elevation and open expanses, became
known for its healthy air and healthy people" begins one chapter in a
local history book. Perhaps no one exemplifies this better than Bertha
Susan Brown of Northfield Road who celebrated her 100th birthday in
December.
Miss Brown is the possessor of the Boston Post Cane award, signifying
her status as the oldest resident of Lunenburg. Coincidentally, her grand-
father, Luke Chase, for whom Chase Road was named, was the first holder
of the Cane.
In fine physical and mental health, Bertha Brown is a treasurehouse
of local history, which she is willing to share with interested listeners.
Miss Brown is a retired elementary school teacher. Her contributions to
society continue even today as she knits and donates articles to charity
organizations.
Lunenburg congratulates and affectionately salutes Bertha Susan Brown.

Board of Selectmen
AUGUST N.MISNER
JAMES I.
ANN P. HALL
Board of Selectmen: Seated Left to right- August N. Misner, Chairman James T. Van Buren and Ann P. Hall.
Standing - Clerk Francelina C. Vaughan and Executive Secretary Barry J. Stell.
For the-Selectmen, 1983 was a year of change and challenge -- change reflected in new personnel who
face a continuing challenge of maintaining a level of expected services with a shrinking revenue base.
Dennis Carrier succeeded Jim Deming as Fire Chief. Al Gauthier was replaced by Leon Langille as
Veterans' Agent. Upon the resignation of Austin Fitzsimmons, Roger Williams was appointed as Building
Inspector. After an extended leave of absence, Rick Eley resigned and Barry Stell became the new Executive
Secretary. In addition, Ed Harriman was appointed Auditor to replace the late Jim Bascom.
We are confident that the new appointees will serve Lunenburg with as much diligence and ability as their
predecessors, whose total service encompassed 122 years.
The challenge of Prop 2½ -- providing services with less money -- continued in 1983. Although the
inflationary spiral slowed, expenses continued to rise in both labor and materials.
Examples abound throughout the town. Although the 1983 Town Meeting expressed a desire to replace
the Holman Street culvert, obtaining the materials and skills necessary has proved impossible within the
dollar amount voted. The police contract for the current fiscal year remains unsettled. Replacing needed
capital equipment continues to task the imagination and resources of both department heads and Board
members.
In an effort to contain costs and increase revenues, the Board has negotiated a new street lighting
agreement; voted new fees for the Sanitary Landfill, Automatic Amusement Devices and Dog Licenses;
auctioned unneeded and unwanted town property; and even shortened the length of this award winning Town
Report.
4
===PAGE 7===
The Board feels strongly that the town cannot afford the services it has come to expect without paying for
the same. Therefore, we have formed ad hoc committees on communications and computers in an attempt to
limit costs while increasing services. Grants have been (and are being) applied for under federal and state
programs to provide and/or replace services for the townspeople.
To preserve the character of our community we must act on our own initiative before that initiative is lost
to outside regulators. We recognize the intrusion of this external regulation into local government has
become a fact of municipal life in the 1980's. For this reason, the Board encourages the town to accept the
provisions of the General Laws allowing for professional management of the town's financial affairs and to
implement the recommendations of the Town Management Study Committee to establish a strong Executive
Secretary and approve a Town Charter Commission.

Auditor
I have examined, for the period July 1, 1983 to December 31, 1983, the books and accounts of the officer of
the Town of Lunenburg who is entrusted with receipt, custody or expenditure of money, including all original
bills and vouchers on which money has been paid from the Treasury. I have also reconciled the Treasurer's
cash balances and investments (see below). My examinations were made in accordance with generally
accepted auditing standards. These present fairly the balances in these accounts as of December 31, 1983.
Consistent with the practices of many municipalities in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, the Town of
Lunenburg has not maintained historical cost records of its property, plant and equipment. I have recom-
mended in a letter to the Board of Selectmen, dated March 6, 1984, that this be done to conform with generally
accepted accounting principles and provide insurable values. During my audit I have made the following
recommendations and they have been implemented to provide better control:
1. A standard weekly payroll time card approved by department heads.
2. Department approval on every original invoice and a standard voucher approval form.
3. Review and account for all pre-printed sequential checks including those cancelled or voided each
month.
4. Correction of inconsistencies on pay for part-time employees on holidays.

Planning Board
Planning Board: Seated - Marion M. Benson and Chairman Charles H. B. Arning. Standing - James A. Mann
and James R. Sharkey. Not present when photo was taken was James M. Sudolnik.
The major goal of the Planning Board is to begin to implement the objectives of the Master Plan as
presented to the Town at the last Annual Town Meeting. We have already taken positive steps to begin this
process.
Lunenburg, in regard to many areas of concern, is at a cross-roads where hard decisions need to be made.
The Planning Board is one of the public boards that is beginning to make these hard choices between
conflicting interests. The process of public hearings is an essential ingredient in the formulation of these
choices. Historically, the Planning Board hearings have been poorly attended. It is critical that the Townspeo-
ple be involved in this decision-making process. In order for the Town to continue to preserve its heritage as a
rural, agricultural community while at the same time being open to innovative opportunities to expand the tax
base and create appropriate housing opportunities, the citizens of Lunenburg must become more involved in
this process.
Our plans for the coming year are to push for a special committee to study the Town Center, not only the
Town Hall, but also the traffic and scenic elements of the Center as well; to conduct a physical survey of the
watershed area as outlined in the Master Plan to increase the zoning from one to two acres in specific
locations where there is a need to further protect our water resources (based on the outcome of the survey);
and if this is passed, to develop a Cluster Zoning By-law which will allow a builder to better utilize large parcels
of acreage for housing opportunities that lessen the impact of sewage and water use on the water resources of
the Town.
The Planning Board signed 22 plans that did not require subdivision approval generating $220 dollars in
revenue for the Town. The Planning Board approved a new subdivision off Flat Hill Road by Mr. Jouko Kulju
called Oakwood Estates. In addition, the Planning Board has approved Canterbury Drive and the subdivision
of the same name.
7
===PAGE 10===
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors: left to right - Secretary Jacqueline A. Keeler, Chairman William F. Reed, Bernard J. Paton
and Kathryn M. Herrick.
The Board of Assessors wishes to thank the property owners of the Town for their cooperation during the
past two years. The year 1982 was hectic with the revaluation of the Town being completed by Robert J.
Finnegan and Associates, Inc. and all the attendant hearings and abatements which were conducted.
The end result of the revaluation was certified as being correct by the Department of Revenue, and based
on the number of requests for abatements, we feel that a proper and equal job of assessing valuations was
done. We have 4,000 parcels of property in the Town, and 212 requests for abatements were filed (5.3%). After
consideration and action on these requests for abatements, 8 taxpayers have appealed our decisions and filed
for appellate tax court hearings.
The main subject of the abatement requests was the revaluation of the land in the Town. The homes and
buildings were not, in the main, overvalued. The problem with the land was that Lunenburg had a long history
of extremely low assessments on land. This was done to enable the taxpayers to hold large tracts of land at
little expense and to maintain the rural character of the Town.
The State Department of Revenue insisted that the law be observed and that all land be assessed at its full
and fair market value. This value was determined by using land sale prices over the years 1979 to 1981, and
these figures established your land values for 1982.
The tax rate for Fiscal Year 1984 was set at $19.92 with the total valuation of the Town being $193,289,258.
We are working under the fiscal restraints of Proposition 2½ without any great reductions in services to you.
That portion of the budget to be raised by taxation can only be increased by 2½% annually, and this law is
forcing local and state governments to look to other sources of funds to continue operaton of municipal
services.
8
===PAGE 11===
We wish to apprise you of the need for a mini-revaluation and certification of the assessed valuation of the
Town for Fiscal Year 1986 which starts July 1, 1985. We are required by law to conduct this evaluation every
three years, and will conduct this revaluation starting January 2, 1985 through June 31, 1985. We will request
the funds via an article at the Annual Town Meeting in May 1984. Upon completion of submission of bids to
this office, we intend to engage a revaluation contractor to provide a field review of each piece of property with
buildings sold during years 1982-1984 and all new construction completed during the same period. This
information will be used to update the current assessed valuations and should satisfy the Department of
Revenue's requirements for recertification.
We do wish to report that housing starts are up and land sales show a steady increase in the cost of
building lots. The average price of a new home is increasing from the mid-fifties to the seventy-thousand-
dollar range. We find that ranch-style homes (single story) are not as popular as story-and-a-half or two-story
homes in new construction being built throughout the Town.
We wish to inform all interested taxpayers that the following clauses of the General Law are availble for
tax relief to taxpayers who qualify. For further information do not hesitate to contact the Assessors' Office.
Clause 17C
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation
or a tax of $175, whichever is greater.
Clause 22
- Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000
valuation or a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of
disability.
Clause 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100%
disability.
Clause 37A
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Clause 41B
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation of a tax of $500, whichever is greater.
Clause 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with gross receipts of under
$20,000 annually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred
payment plan, the taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Clause 42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000
valuation or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 43
- Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation
or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Chapter 61A
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its
agricultural or horticultural use.
Chapter 61
- Provides for assessment of forest land managed under a planned program with certifi-
Chapter 61B
cation of State Forester.
Recreational Land
(1) Provides for assessment of land not less than five acres if it is retained in substan-
tially a natural, wild or open condition or in a landscaped condition in such a manner as
to allow to a significant extent the preservation of wildlife and other natural resources.
(2) Land deemed to be recreational land which is devoted primarily to recreational use
and is available to the general public or members of a non-profit organization.

Park Commission
The past year was one of evaluation, decision and failure. We took a long hard look at our facilities,
equipment, operative policies, progams and financial resources. It was clearly evident that the Park Depart-
ment was approaching a point where salary expense and maintenance costs would result in the elimination of
recreational activities. We made a decision not to cut or eliminate recreational programs, but to control costs
and seek additional funding. We recognized that the greatest resource of any community is its youth and that
the money had to be channeled into recreational programs. To accomplish this service, charges were
increased and user fees were proposed.
In an attempt to upgrade our facilities, we applied for a federal grant to add facilities and repair current
ones. Our application failed to attract much needed dollars. The town beach house is in need of repair and our
emphasis at town meetings will be to acquire these funds. We continue to apply bandaids where major surgery
is needed and we will pursue all alternatives to upgrade our facilities.
We would like to take this opportunity to acknowledge those individuals who make this all possible. We
would like to thank Greg Bingham for his time and effort and we all wish him well in his new career endeavor.
Tom Leppala has replaced Greg and in his first six months has demonstrated his creativity and initiative. Jerry
Beloin, the town beach director, supervised our highly visible beach program. Usage of this town beach was
up 20% from the prior year. The summer recreation program administered by Dick Stachowicz continued to
flourish. The playground programs, day trips,summer basketball and arts & crafts provided our youth with
needed recreational activities. Ron Lamothe supervised the winter basketball program with continued
widespread success. To all those who have volunteered their time, effort and in some cases money, we extend
to you our grateful appreciation. We are committed to provide the community with quality programs, services
and facilities.
The Park Commissioners are: Chairman Joseph A. Giovino, Martha M. Johnson and Robert A. Membrino.

===PAGE 14===
Housing Authority
Housing Authority: left to right - Lance D. May, Chairman Ella Rae Denault, Josephine C. Meloon, Executive
Secretary Eileen M. Alleva and Richard I.White. Not present when photo was taken was Judith Tarbell.
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its 14th year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street,
known as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently, we have forty-three apartments occupied by
single persons, and five occupied by couples.
The tenants have organized the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association which meets on a regular basis and
arranges interesting projects for the benefit and enjoyment of it members. Mr. Eugene Deslauriers holds the
office of President and is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
The tenants are provided with weekly transportation. The shut-ins are provided with "Meals-on Wheels"
and the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health provides the monthly Well-Oldster Clinic for the tenants. Home
Health Care provides household duties and shopping.
The requirements for occupancy are that the applicants be at least sixty-five years of age and in need of
good housing. Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of income can be charged for rent which includes
heat, water, and electricity. The rising cost of energy makes it very difficult for the Authority to keep within
their budget.
We invite anyone who is interested to inquire at the Housing Authority Office at 131 White Street, where
Mrs. Eileen Alleva, Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions. You may call 345-7655 or
342-5308.
12
===PAGE 15===
Library Trustees
Library Trustees: Seated left to right - Director Elizabeth H. Burnap, Chairman Edward A. O'Brien and Sheila
A. Jalutkewicz. Standing left to right- Kathleen M. Pliska, Thomas R. Rossi and June M. Dettenrieder. Not
present when photo was taken was Carleton A. Wollenberg.
In 1983, the Trustees and staff of Ritter Memorial Library received a very ambiguous message from
Townspeople. The budget restrictions that were voted, in spite of rising costs, seemed to indicate that we must
conserve, think small", and perhaps reduce services. Yet the demand for services continued to increase as it
has every year for the past several, stretching resources to the limit. There were but two full-time employees
and six part-time, including the custodian, to cover forty-eight open hours, and to provide every age level in
the community with complete library services in so far as possible.
In July, we were able to increase staff time by ten very important hours, although it was touch and go for
months. The necessary funds were reported to be forthcoming at the state level and then cancelled several
times. When the Department of Revenue failed to specify that this supplementary aid was for libraries, most
towns in Massachusetts did not use it for that purpose. Lunenburg was very fortunate in having a Town
Treasurer who made a point of having this extra aid returned to the library budget. Continuation of state aid at
this increased level will be essential to maintain present library services in 1984.
Generous gifts from the Lunenburg Memorial Fund in memory of Arthur Brockelman, Bernard Quinlan,
and Dr. James Congdon have been of great assistance in the past year. Books were given by individuals in
memory of Roberta Rutledge, George Schaff, Dr. Charles E. Woods, Lawrence Sudolnik, Susan Gelinas,
Robert Larkin, Helen Flynn, and Dr. James Congdon. Additional materials, both new and used, were donated
by many persons. The total circulation for 1983 was 54,230, divided as follows: adult, 22,903: young adult,
1,469;and children, 29,858--an increase of nearly 32% in the past four years.
13
===PAGE 16===
The choice of large print books available to library patrons has been substantially increased through a
grant awarded Leominster Library, in conjunction with Lunenburg, Lancaster, Clinton and Sterling. This
augmented the large print books borrowed from the Worcester Regional system and offered local readers a
much more varied selection.
The many gifts and services of the Friends of the Library were an important factor in helping us to meet the
public need and yet live within our budget. They renewed passes to Boston Museum of Fine Arts and the
Aquarium, and purchased a new Science Museum pass. The Friends sponsored "Third Thursday" programs
each month, including an antique book appraiser, International Nights, a folk singer, and a talk on gardening.
They provided refreshments, publicity and volunteer help for these events, as well as for children's story hours
and for the 9th annual Children's Art Festival. They paid for performers during the summer reading program
and for books awarded to the children. Fund raising events included a yard sale, "Book & Bake" sale, a
boutique table and a Christmas cookie sale. Rental books, copies of best-sellers, were provided by the
Friends. This self-supporting service was popular with readers who would rather rent a book for a few days
than wait for the library copy.
New borrowers' cards were issued to every library patron, with the exception of pre-school children,
starting in March. The previous cards had been used since 1976 and the files were overflowing. From March
through December, 2, 196 cards, good for three years, were issued.
Children's services have been of increasing importance for several years, and demand for them acceler-
ated last year. Regular story hours for a capacity registration were conducted for pre-schoolers, as were
monthly movies. Each kindergarten and first grade class visited the library every other week. Children of all
ages used the library for research and assistance with every type of school assignment, as well as to obtain
materials for their own enjoyment. The value of the library as a warm, safe place where one could "borrow the
phone" and wait for one's "ride" was obvious to anyone visiting in mid-afternoon! A most successful "Author
Day" was held in April, with authors John Bellairs and Georgess McHargue visiting the library and Middle
School to discuss their work with all interested children. This annual event was co-sponsored by the library
and the school, and funded in part by the State Arts Lottery. In the summer of 1983, the reading program, with
a dinosaur-hunting theme, attracted 150 enthusiastic children who read 3,990 books, and enjoyed weekly
programs and contests.
The years 1984-85 will mark the 75th anniversary of the building of Ritter Memorial Library. Several
special events are planned and all townspeople are invited to join in the celebration.

===PAGE 17===
Fire Department
During 1983, the Department responded to 425
calls, a slight decrease from 1982 totals. The calls
were as follows: Structure - 49; Investigation - 18;
Motor Vehicle - 16; Brush - 15; Telephonic - 13;
Mutual Aid - 14; Box Alarms - 12; Accident Standby -
12; Needless - 7; Illegal Burning - 4; Rubbish - 4;
Service - 3; Outside Electrical - 2; Water/Ice Rescue
- 2; Chemical Spill - 1; Lawn Mower - 1; Ambulance
Assist - 1; Washdown - 1; Train - 1; Aircraft Landing
1; Rescue/Ambulance 248.
Chimney fires are included in the Structure fire
category. We had 28 chimney fires this year, the
same as in 1982. These fires are very dangerous due
to the concealed spaces that a chimney occupies.
We cannot see what is burning and this presents a
possibility of structural damage to a home. Towns-
people must properly maintain their chimneys,
stovepipes and woodstoves, cleaning chimneys at
least twice a year and stovepipes at least every four
to six weeks of stove operation.
This Department is fortunate to have fifteen to
eighteen people responding to daytime alarms.
Most area Departments have trouble getting
enough manpower to fight daytime fires, because
their people work out of town. In Lunenburg, town
departments release our firefighters when a fire call
comes in, including our younger members that
come from the High School. These young people
Dennis M. Carrier, Fire Chief
are very well trained and are a valuable asset to our
Department. The School Department is to be com-
mended for their assistances to the Fire Department
and the town by allowing this unique service. Ninety
per cent of our firefighters today started their fire-
fighting careers while they were in High School.
In 1983, fourteen hundred manhours were spent for firefighter training, an increase of 20% over 1982. This
training was done both in town and out of town using instructors from our department and area instructors. Of
the total time spent in training, less than 25% was paid by the town, the rest being donated by the firefighters.
This is an indication of the dedication of our call firefighters. All firefighters are also certified in CPR and
Advanced First Aid.
Our ambulance is staffed with eighteen firefighters who are also Emergency Medical Technicians
(EMT's). In additon to their fire training, EMT's, who must re-certify with the State every two years, require an
additional twelve hundred manhours of training per year in the medical field.
Effective in December 1983, the town received a much higher rating from the Insurance Services Office.
This organization rates municipalities and determines fire insurance rates. These new, lower rates were
achieved due to multiple improvements in the water system; the Department's handling of incoming alarms;
the Department equipment, organization and response times. With the addition of our large diameter hose, we
were able to demonstrate to the Insurance Services Office that we could give hydrant protection up to 3,000
feet (vs. 500') from the last hydrant in the system, one of the largest extensions of hydrant coverage in the
country.
15
===PAGE 18===
This year, the Department is asking the town to replace our main pumper truck, a 1961 model that is
unreliable due to corrosion, breakdowns and the unavailability of parts. This truck carries one half of our large
diameter hose and is our primary water supply truck. With costs of fire apparatus going up 15-20% per year
and the critical need of this particular truck in our service to you, we feel that it is prudent and necessary to
replace this unit at this time, especially since we've put off this request for three years.
My first year as Chief of the Fire Department has been a hectic and busy one. I would like to take this
opportunity to thank all the members of the Department for their support and their efforts during the year. I
would also like to thank town officials and department heads for their help and understanding. Also, thanks to
the townspeople for your support both on the Town Meeting floor and throughout the year. Without this
cooperation, we would have great difficulty poviding fire protection to the town.
I would also like to especially thank retired Fire Chief James P. Deming for his help during my first year of
leadership in the Department and for the many years of service and leadership that he has given to our
Department.

Police Department
Police Department: Seated left to right - John E. Baker, Police Chief Hector J. Morin, Jr. and Sergeant Richard
L. Lambert, Jr. Standing left to right - Charles P. Deming, Jr., Alphonse J. Baron, George E. Aho, Steven G.
Whitney, Daniel F. Bourgeois, David S. Delisle, James P. Marino and Leo J. Janssens, II.
In the year 1983, Lunenburg showed a 31% decrease in Crimes Against People and Property following
along with the National trend. Excessive speed and alcohol (a deadly combination), were responsible for five
of the eight motor vehicle fatalities recorded in Lunenburg for the year 1983. An extensive Speed Enforcement
Program was established by the Police Department along with two new moving radar units that were put into
the cruisers. Target areas in the community were patrolled heavily in an attempt to reduce speeding and
personal injury accidents. Community involvement was very good with calls being received by the Police
Department, listing times and places on other problem areas. Hopefully, this joint effort between the commun-
ity and the Police will continue in 1984, and together we can make the streets of Lunenburg a safer place to
drive and the town a safer place to live.
Through a joint effort of members of the Department, we were able to obtain a $6,000 grant from the
Governor's Highway Safety Council to establish a Drunk Driving/Speed Enforcement Program. Program
started on January 13, 1984 and will continue through June 1, 1984.
Training was given a high priority this year with all regular members of the Department participating in
Radar Training, Accident Investigation, Crime Scene Search, Drug and Alcohol Abuse and Crime Prevention
courses. As a result, members of the force are working with the schools and the students on Drug and Alcohol
Awareness Programs.
The Department has established a Crime Prevention Program and will be conducting home/business
security surveys for those people who would like to participate. Statistics show that you must have three
things occur; DESIRE to commit the crime, ABILITY to perpetrate it and OPPORTUNITY. By eliminating the
opportunity, you have eliminated the crime. I hope that the people of our community will take the time to call
the Police Station and set up an appointment with our Crime Prevention officer. If SO, you will be surprised how
with very little effort and very little money (sometimes, no money), you can discourage someone from
attempting to break into your home and eliminate whatever loss might have occurred.

Highway Department
America's roads are falling apart. Half the coun-
try's paved roads will require major reconstruction
by the end of the decade, but Highway and road
repair and maintenance funds simply have not kept
pace with the deterioration. The states' meager
maintenance funds are spent on newer streets and
roads at the expense of the older ones. Many of the
older roads have deteriorated to a point beyond
maintenance help, and require rehabilitation.
Potholes and other highway deterioration lead to
greater deterioration at a faster pace, longer gas
mileage, damage vehicles, and add generally to veh-
icles' wear-and-tear.
Much of the Federal and State funds are derived
from gasoline taxes. Over the years, repair and
maintenance funds have shrunk because gasoline
consumption has been declining in recent years. As
the price has gone up, many motorists have curbed
their driving. At the same time, newer vehicles get
better mileage, requiring less gasoline. And, high
inflation in construction costs has shrunk the
amount of construction that can be financed from
existing highway taxes.
Skimpy budgets often translate into minimal
highway and street repair and have precipitated a
search for new methods and technologies to
improve upon the costly total reconstruction
system.
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
Once a crack in a road develops, moisture seeps through to eventually erode the sub-base material
supporting the concrete or asphalt road. After a couple of season, including several freezes and thaws,
thermal contraction and expansion eat away the pavement, leaving a pothole. In the past the only effective
solution was Highway Reconstruction. The pavement is broken up and hauled away, the roadbed is leveled
and smoothed and new supports laid, then the new pavement is laid. Because of cost, most repair attempts -
involving a waterproof sealer and an asphalt overlay - are much less complicated and costly. But neither the
sealer nor the overlay treat the basic structural problems involved in reflective cracking.
New repair methods are already in use in many areas of the country, with the emphasis on lower cost and
less interruption of traffic flow. One such system uses high-strength glass fiber reinforced fabric to cover
cracks. This Department has also experimented with a "Chipseal" process to repair Lunenburg's roads. We
hope these new methods are approved by the State authorities as they will speed road maintenance in the
future as well as stretch their budget dollars allocated for street repair.
Labor: We lost two men during this year because of disabilities. $157,823.23 was spent for labor performing
all aspects of highway related work.
Chapter 90: Work was performed under three separate chapters of State Funds. Drainage and resurfacing
was completed on Electric, Chase, Lancaster, West Groton, Highland and School Streets at a cost of
$101,045.77
General Higway Maintenance: 39,250 gallons of asphalt was applied to Gilchrest, Goodrich, Arbor, Elm-
wood, Flat Hill, Rolling Acres, Meadow Lane, New W. Townsend, Kilburn, Lincoln, Pine Grove, Cushing,
Valley, Turkey Hill and Northfield Roads at a cost of $41,021.13. 248.40 Tons of patch cost $6,236.25.

===PAGE 22===
Catchbasin cleaning, street signs, cutting brush, purchasing of guardrail, drainage materials, small tools
come under this account.
Snow Removal: There wasn't the usual amount of snowfall this winter, but severe ice conditions prevailed,
causing considerable use of salt and sand. A total of $54,342.86 was spent to keep our roads free from snow
and ice some $27,755 less than the previous year.
Drainage Expense: Drainage projects were completed on Electric Avenue, Youngs, Howard, Page and
Chestnut Streets at a cost of $15,979.15.

under this account, plus the purchase of plow & grader blades, chains, steel for fabricating truck beds, small
tools, oxygen and acetylene, gas, grease and oil. A total of $67,507.57 was spent to keep the equipment in
good condition.
Town Barn Maintenance: Heat, light, telephone, janitorial and maintenance supplies, stationery supplies &
postage, office equipment and repairs, special building maintenance and Superintendent's expense account
cost $9,556.45. Heat is turned off in April until late fall in an effort to keep costs down.

account diminishes. $4,262.83 was spent under this account. Highland Street Reconstruction was completed
at a cost of $36,923.96.

Conservation Commission
Conservation Commission: Seated left to right - Robert A. Garneau, Chairman Raymond G. Chapdelaine and

The rapid rise in the demand for new homes resulted in the busiest year ever for your Conservation
Commission. A record number of Wetland Hearings were held in 1983. Because of a loophole in the zoning
laws, houses were built on proper sized lots that were partially submerged under wetlands and in some cases
under streams and ponds. The Planning Board recommended a By-law change at the February Special Town
Meeting that remedied this problem.
Your Commission wishes to thank the townspeople for their support in 1983 and also that you continue to
help us in the task of protecting our environment.

Council on Aging
Council on Aging: Seated left to right - Doris M. Carrier, Helen M. White, Martha L. Mologhan and Elvira Z.

Our first newsletter in over a year was published this past November, utilizing a computerized mailing list
and the efforts of Dorothy Schlicter (typing) and Norma Wasile of the United Parish (printing).
Christmas Dinner featured a new look as The Dads and Grandads came to serenade and give us a chance
to sing along. Martha Molaghan and friends served delicious refreshments.
The Council assisted the Friendly Seniors Bus Trips program with a grant of $1,200. In addition, we were
again able to utilize the services of Einard Warpula's company for our twice weekly and special lift vehicle
program, both of which seem to fit our present needs.
The Wednesday Drop-In Center program (10 AM-1 PM) continues to grow, with many of the tasks
formerly performed by Marcy Peed now handled by Chet Pliska, Doris Lahti and many other volunteers.
Sandra Flanagan and her crew continue to deliver hot lunches each weekday (except holidays) to seniors
unable to prepare meals for themselves.
This year we were able to direct some funds to purchase a file cabinet, typewriter and stand to increase
our efficiency. In addition, we returned 15% of our budget ($3,500) to the Town's General Fund from user fees
on the transportation and hot lunch programs.
We have been pleased that Father Edward Moran of St. Boniface Church and Bill Dahlgren have come on
to the Council this past year. We do miss the gentle, smiling ways of Mark Thatcher.
We hope that our efforts will continue to encourage and help the town Seniors who are such a vital force
for good in our community.

Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings
In 1983 there were 183 inspections made rela-
tive to plumbing and gas fitting permits. All new
house construction require a rough test and finish
inspection before it is approved for occupancy.
Sixty-six inspections were made for new homes, 29
for alterations/remodeling, 15 for commercial build-
ings and 11 inspections for solar hot water installa-
tions. Out of the 11 inspections for solar hot water, 5
were found to be in violation of Chapter 142 of the
State Plumbing Code. I strongly urge homeowners
who are considering solar hot water to insist that the
installer have a plumbing permit before commenc-
ing the work. Solar hot water tanks and "energy
saving" hot water heaters are getting very popular
due to the high demand by the consumer for lower
fuel costs for water heating.
There were 57 gas fitting permits issued in 1983
-- 38 for various gas appliances, 6 for commercial
buildings and 13 for liquefied petrolem gas installa-
tions. Natural gas, as an energy saver, is still in big
demand in this area, but because of the constant
rise in gas prices, these installations seem to be
Thomas E. Mortis, Inspector of Plumbing & Gas
leveling off to some extent.
Fittings
I make note that the Board of Examiners of Plumbers and Gas Fitters has approved the use of some types
of automatic vent dampers, but the use of unvented gas room heaters is still under investigation by the Board.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
The Inspector of Weights & Measures com-
pleted 35 scale inspections and 34 gasoline pump
inspections. Minor adjustments were required in
two cases. All pumps and scales that were inspected
were sealed. The total revenue from this work was
$577.00.
A point of interest to the public who do business
involving the use of scales and pumps, whether it be
for selling or for purchasing. It is well to note that the
green colored seals are used for the even numbered
years and the red seals are used for the odd num-
bered years. This is in accordance with State
regulations.
Any suggestions and comments on the opera-
tion of this Department will be welcomed.
Lauren W. Davies, Inspector of Weights & Measures

Wiring Inspector
The office of Wiring Inspector experienced a
year of growth in 1983. Coinciding with the local and
national economic recovery, this office issued 142
wiring permits which resulted in approximately 300
inspections. These increased numbers demonstrate
that economic recovery has hit the local area.
The Wiring Inspector's duties include inspec-
tions of wiring installations for residential, commer-
cial and industrial buildings, swimming pools,
electric services and other related electrical work.
All wiring must conform to the latest edition of the
Massachusetts Electric Code.
A fee is required for all wiring permits & inspec-
tions. The permits are issued at the Town Clerk's
office. Current fee schedules are available from the
Clerk's Office.
At this time I would like to thank the Building
Inspector, Plumbing Inspector and the Fire Chief for
their assistance and cooperation during the year.
James R. Sharkey, Inspector of Wires
Tree Warden
As predicted, the caterpillar invasion of the past
few years was greatly diminished. Because of the
continuation of Proposition 2½, the State Dept. of
Natural Resources has ceased reimbursing Towns a
percentage of expenditures, nor does it give a
week's work tagging trees and diseased tree remo-
val as was the practice in previous years. Townspeo-
ple could take their brush to the Town Garage for
chipping on the second and fourth Saturdays of the
month, but not many people took advantage of this
service.

A toatal of $8,000.00 was spent under the Dutch
Elm Account and $6,698.66 was spent under the
Tree Warden Account.
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden

Animal Control Officer
It seems that every year my duties should
decrease, but it doesn't seem to be turning out that
way. Every year has been an experience, but as
Animal Control Officer and Animal Inspector it
seems that my life is never boring. I have had a very
rewarding and busy year. The two most important
things that I have accomplished this year are a 95%
placement for our stray dogs and cats, and an inde-
pendence from the County which enables monies
pertaining to animals to remain right here in Lunen-
burg, hopefuly helping to make my department self-
supporting.
I received 2,400 calls this year. Some of these
calls were related to dogs and cats, while many were
about wild animals. I also received numerous calls
dealing with large animals - horses, deer, goats, pigs
and cows. The amount of injured animals was on the
rise and thanks to the Fallon Animal Hospital and
their staff, all injured animals had a fair shake at
living. Most of the calls I responded to personally,
while others were handled by telephone.
I took three courses toward my degree in
Animal Control and was elected as secretary to the
Board of the University of New Hampshire's New
England Humane Academy. This is a two year term.
Kathleen M. Comeau, Animal Control Officer
Stray and abandoned animals are still a great problem in Lunenburg, but after much investigation it
seems that the number of strays is very low compared to many other communities. Changing fees for
unspayed and unaltered dogs will hopefully help to correct some of this situation while helping to decrease
the number of unwanted newborn puppies.
I still receive much help and support from Massachusetts Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals
and appreciate their help very much.
My licensing campaign has worked well and I didn't have to write as many fines this year. I'm still hoping
and working toward 100% licensing.
I would like to take this time to thank all the people who are so important to me in my position as Animal
Control Officer - the Selectmen and staff, Town Counsel, Town Clerk and staff, Fire Department and of
course, our Police Chief and all our Police Officers and Dispatchers who help me so much. Because of them,
my job is a little easier.
Please feel free to call me for any problem large or small. If I am out, a message will be taken and I will
return your call. I can't promise to solve every problem, but I certainly will try.

Town Management Study Committee Report
ARTICLE 32 of the Lunenburg Annual Town Meeting Warrant, June 1983 authorized the Board of
Selectmen and the Town Moderator to appoint a five member study committee to investigate the
establishment of a Selectmen-Town Manager form of government and to consider expansion of the Board of
Selectmen, the establishment of a separate Board of Health, or any other change to the executive branch it
deemed advisable; said committee to report back to the next Annual Town Meeting, provided that at least
thirty days prior to Town Meeting an informative public hearing is held. Pursuant to this vote, the following
persons were appointed by the Selectmen and the Moderator to serve on such committee: Diane J. Allen,
Melissa D. Cobb, Herbert E. Dunnington, Walter B. Keeler, and James W. Rockefeller.
In the course of its study, our Committee met with the Selectmen and most other town boards and
committees to solicit their input and suggestions relative to professional management for the town.
Additionally, we met with Town Managers from the towns of Acton and Winchendon. We collected data and
materials from the Executive Office of Communities and Development, Division of Community Services of the
Commonwealth of Massachusetts, along with much pertinent information from other towns.
CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS
The ever growing complexities of town government are resulting in a time and energy commitment
beyond the scope of part-time administrators. We find that our Selectmen spend approximately twenty hours
a week carrying out routine administrative responsibilities, leaving little time for long-range planning or for
research necessary for informed decision making. We conclude that some relief should be forthcoming as
soon as possible.
This committee believes that the "Town Manager with a Strong Charter" form of government would give
our townspeople the maximum benefit and maximum efficiency. This was also the finding of the study
committee of 1966-67 on the same subject. An analogy, we think, is the professional administrator that School
Committees employ in the form of a School Superintendent. It is difficult to imagine a school committee
administering a school system, yet we ask part-time Selectmen to administer the major balance of town
government.
Our research indicated that there were three avenues to the establishment of an administrative
management post in local town government:
1.
Establishing the position of Executive Secretary, Town Administrator or Town Manager by
securing the adoption of a Home Rule Charter.
2.
Securing the Adoption of a Special Act of the Legislature.
3.
Adopting a Bylaw or Vote of the Town Meeting, thus establishing the Executive Secretary
position as found in Massachusetts General Laws, which provides as follows:
SECTION 23A CHAPTER 41: A town may by vote or by-law, authorize and empower the select-
men to appoint an executive secretary who may be appointed by them for a term of one or three
years and to remove him at their discretion. An executive secretary appointed under the pro-
visions of this section shall be sworn to the faithful performance of his duties. During the time
that he holds office he shall hold no elective town office, but he may be appointed by the select-
men or, with the approval, by any other town officer, board, committee or commission, to any
other town office or position consistent with his office. He shall receive such aggregate compen-
sation, not exceeding the amount appropriated therefor, as the selectmen may determine. He
shall act by and for the selectmen in any matter which they may assign to him relating to the
administration of the affairs of the town or of any town office or department under their super-
vision and control, or, with the approval of the selectmen, may perform such other duties as may
be requested of him by any other town officer, board, committee or commission. (1956, 145).
28
===PAGE 31===
An immediate form of relief would be the adoption of option three, Section 23A Chapter 41 of the General
Laws as previously outlined. This would allow the selectmen to legally delagate any of their lawful duties to the
executive secretary. This form is generally known as the "Weak Town Manager" form and has been and is
being employed by more and more of the towns in our size and population category. The plan has the obvious
advantage of relieving the part-time selectmen of a tangle of administrative details thereby freeing time to
devote to the study of major town problems and long-range planning. The executive secretary can assist the
selectmen by supplying information relative to questions the board must act upon. The executive secretary
may also have a particular value as a unifying force in the town government by representing an important
channel of communications between the board of selectmen and town officials and citizens.
This committee also feels the town should consider the establishment of a Charter Commission to work
toward the adoption of a "Home Rule Charter." This commission would look at the entire town government
along with Lunenburg's by-laws and recommend to the town changes and additions that would maximize the
efficiency and services of local government. Under the provision of the Home Rule Amendment, Article
LXXXIX of the Massachusetts Constitution, any city or town, upon petition of 15% of the registered voters, may
vote to elect a nine member charter commission to form a new charter. During the ten month, or eighteen
month, period after its election, the commission prepares a comprehensive charter document establishing the
framework for the administration and re-organizaiton of local government structure. To become effective, a
charter proposal must be adopted by a majority electorate at the next annual town election. We feel this
process would take approximatley two to three years to accomplish.
With regard to increasing the membership of the Board of Selectmen and any change in the Board of
Health, we recommend that such changes be reconsidered at a later date, if warranted, after the establishment
of executive secretary or a Charter Commission.
This committee wishes to thank the Board of Selectmen for the opportunity to serve in this study and
thank all who contributed to our efforts.

Montachusett Regional Vocational
Technical School District
Nineteen hundred eighty-three was a banner year for Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
School in many ways.
Graduation in June was held in the Field House at Mount Wachusett Community College. Two hundred
graduates with parents, friends and faculty attended the impressive ceremony. An honorary vocational
certificate was presented to Morrill Sprague, school committee member from Harvard, an individual who has
been outstandingly supportive of Vocational Education and Monty Tech over many years.
Five hundred twenty-five applications were received for grade nine, three hundred thirty were accepted,
and thus the largest freshman class in the history of the school entered. The total student enrollment on
October 1, 1983, was one thousand fifty-nine.
Students have been active in the National Honor Society, the Vocational and Industrial Clubs of America
(VICA), the Vocational Programs Advisory comittees, two Bloodmobile drives, and collection of "Quarters for
Charity". Two hundred twenty-six pints of blood were donated to the Red Cross. Over $800 was collected in
the annual "Quarters for Christmas" drive and will be divided equally between the towns of Harvard and
Sterling. The District has reason to be proud of the recognition of merit of the Monty Tech students who won
many awards during the year.
The Food Service students took a trip to Bermuda during which they had the opportunity to tour
Stonington Junior College, Paget, Bermuda, where a special program was presented for their benefit. All
costs were paid by the students who earned the money through school activities.
Federal grant money, received through the Department of Education, made possible the purchase of new
equipment which would have been otherwise inaccesible. A new computer room with eighteen micro
computers is now available for students. To maximize the use of this room, a program on computer usage was
instituted for the faculty. During the summer, a program was offered for District students which included
utilization of these computers and other new equipment, and allowed those interested students to have
hands-on experience in selected vocational fields.
Three evening programs were held for the public during the year: Parents' Night, Career Awareness
Night, and our Annual Open House. All were well attended by enthusiastic throngs who appeared keenly
interested in the programs taught, the equipment used, and the work being performed by the students.
Kind donors continue to donate useful gifts to the school. Matthews Chevrolet of Fitchburg and Mathieu
Ford of Winchendon each loaned cars for use in the Driver Education course. Other industrial and business
firms made contributions.
Plans for expansion to include the three towns of Orange, Petersham and Westminster have continued to
occupy much of the District Committe's time. Final articles for town warrants have been submitted, and if
approved, the District will be comprised of two cities and eleven towns.
Once again, the students, staff and District Committee extend their gratitudes to all of the cities and towns
of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District for their continuing support of the
standards and goals of the school.
Listed-below are the member cities and towns and their representative:
Ashby - Jack W. Wescott
Harvard - Morrill G. Sprague
Barre - Francis J. Cranston, Jr.

Town Forest Committee
Town Forest Committee: Seated left to right - Armas S. J. Heino, Chairman Scott W. Foster and Robert A.
Garneau.
The Town Forest Committee has continued, during the past year, to study the feasibility and methods of
managing the Town Forest off Gilchrest Street. The services of a professional forester were investigated.
Interviews were held with three individuals, one of whom, Bob Leupold, was chosen to aid the Committee.
Preliminary studies of the Gilchrest Street forest indicate the need for systematic thinning of crowded
growth and also for removal of poor or diseased trees. The conclusion of Mr. Leupold and the Committee was
that our need is to begin with a test program on a twenty to thirty acre plot.
The goal of the Committee is to have such a plot marked for thinning and ready to be put out to bid by
August, 1984. One independent contractor will be chosen to cut the plot in the fall of 1984. Cordwood will be
the only product harvested.
Proceeds from the first attempt, and subsequent ones, are earmarked for the surveying of existing town
owned lands, controlling erosion on and stabilizing existing forest roads, and payment for the forest
consultant.

Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
It is with pleasure that I submit my first report as Town
Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer.
EDUCATION
Through completion of the first year of the certification
processes for Treasurer-Collectors as well as Town Clerks,
and through monthly meetings, seminars and schools, much
information is gained for the Town's benefit. More impor-
tant, a network is being set up providing a resource which
can be called upon to help deal with the unusual situations
which seem to occur on a regular basis.
APPOINTED vs. ELECTED OFFICIALS
More and more communities are opting to appoint many
officials which they used to elect, their reasoning being
that the appointive process would assure the selection of a
more qualified person. What appears to have been overlooked
is that the independence of the officials in performing
their duties must remain an important goal.
It seems that the well-intentioned should have more faith in
the ability of their electorate to choose best. Their
Robert J. Ebersole
community would be better served if they were to devote
their energies to seeing that elected officials receive full
support in their efforts to improve his or her expertise.
Food for thought for the voters in any community is the indisputable fact that each time they
choose to have appointed an official whom they had previously elected, they surrender a piece
of their share of the democratic process, and reduce the responsiveness of those in office to
the needs and desires of the people.
STAFFING
Due to action at the June Town Meeting, the Administra-
tion budget for the three offices was cut approximately
111½, translating into staff cuts, although services
have not yet had to be reduced. The personnel that
remains is greatly appreciated for their efforts during
the transition period, as well as adjusting to an

better service to townspeople.
TOWN DEBT
Massachusetts cities and towns have a low debt burden as compared to the national average. Lunen-
burg's burden is even lower than the state's, primarily because the Town does not operate either
a water or sewer system.
35
===PAGE 38===
'As the Town's debt has been retired, the corresponding amount paid for principal and interest has
fallen. The money not so spent has been diverted to regular operating budgets. While this has
helped to avoid reductions in services, it has been done at the expense of the Town's
infrastructure, ie. maintenance of the roads, vehicles and buildings. Many towns automatically
appropriate a certain percentage of their total budget for capital improvements and debt service.
Lunenburg shouldnot be afraid to use its borrowing capacity to fund necessary projects.

The Town has the authority to tax itself an additional amount, over and above the Prop. 2½ limit,
to repay debt on capital projects. This may be done only by written ballot at a regularly
scheduled Town or State Election. Of course, any voted override is paid by the taxpayers in Town.
FREE CASH
Free cash, or more correctly, "Fund Balance Available for Appropriation", is the total amount of
money left over at the end of the Town's fiscal year, after deducting outstanding taxes and other
encumbrances. Each year Lunenburg continues to spend part of this to offset the budget.
The Town does not spend all of the free cash, as is true in private industry which maintains
reserves against future contingencies instead of paying dividends (reducing taxes) or spending it
(increasing budgets). It has been recommended that a community maintain between 3% and 5% of its
budget as free cash. The amount retained after the 1983 Annual Town Meeting was $220,883, or 3.5%
of the $6,370,481 budget voted.
Not only does this provide for a fund for emergencies, it also provides a source of income to the
Town in the form of interest. In the event that the tax bills are late due to revaluation delays
(as occured in FY 83), it allows the Town to postpone or not require borrowing, thereby saving
interest payments.
One of the major sources of free cash is that amount of free cash which was not spent in a prior
year. It is a NON-RECURRING RESOURCE, and, if spent, is gone forever. In essence, it is a savings
account. If the Town uses its savings to pay its light bill, the savings will be gone, and next
monththe light bill will be there again. It is better to restrict the growth of the budget and pay
bills out of current income, than to spend out of savings and hurt Lunenburg's security.
PROP 2½, THE VOTER AND EDUCATION
Since this tax cutting referendum was passed, the law has been fine tuned SO as to correct some of
the inequities which were in the original law. It is important to realize that while one provision
of 2½ placed a limit on the tax rate at $25.00, there was another limit imposed. A Town may not
increase its total levy more than 2½ over the prior year. This is a vital part of the law to
assure that cities and towns cannot arbitrarily and WITHOUT VOTER APPROVAL, increase taxes by
inflating the valuation base through revaluation. In fact, just as there may be a vote to override
2½, the law allows the town to vote to order a CUT in the tax levy. It is also significant to note
that for FY 83, 48% of the cities and towns in Massachusetts had a tax rate under $20.00. The
focus of the Town should not always be on the total amount that the Town may tax, but also to look
for ways to increasestate aid, local receipts and federal aid, as well as to determine what
services are necessary for the town to provide.
On the subject of state aid, Chapter 70 assistance to schools is often claimed to belong to the
schools. However, such assistance is merely the conduit through which the state distributes aid to
communities. Officials in the State House admit (albeit reluctantly) that this route was chosen
to make it look as if the current administration is fulfilling its campaign promises to give aid to
schools. However, in reality this aid is. only LOCAL REVENUE SHARING, to be spent as the Town sees
fit. As an alternative, the state would do well by sending additional assistance directly to the
schools without reducing aid to cities and towns, which in turn would further reduce reliance on
36
===PAGE 39===
the property tax to fund education. In fact, by reviewing pre and post-2½ state aid to education
figures, it reveals that the state is picking up a greater share of school costs.
UNFUNDED PENSION LIABILITY
Due to growing concern for the unfunded pension liability in the State, which has been estimated
at 9 to 12 BILLION DOLLARS, as well as the perceived abuse of disability retirements, the Legisla-
ture passed two new laws which may help address the problem. The first made uniform the rules and
regulations that apply to the 105 retirement boards across the state, and provides for a more
impartial medical review system. The second law increased the amount of money available by
increasing employee contributions, expanding investment opportunities and reducing interest paid
to persons who leave the system before retirement age.
While this is an encouraging turn of events, there is much more that needs to be done to control
pension costs. One important recommendation currently under review, is a tightening of the Heart
and Lung provisions for police and fire personnel, by requiring participation in an ongoing physical
fitness health program. Lunenburg should continue to contribute to the fund set up in 1982 to
offset the Town's share of this continuing liability. As was stated at that time, eventually the
Town will be able to appropriate the interest gained, to reduce the amount that would otherwise
have to be raised by taxation.
THE FUTURE
Where do we go from here? The Federal deficit,
worldwide economics, the stock market, state
politics, regional growth
all these play a
ECONOMIC INDICATORS
role in Lunenburg's future. Continued financial
responsibility on the part of Town officials in
the budgetary process, as well as a spirit of
communication with the public as to the needs of
the Town and the resources available, will
assure that Lunenburg's outstanding financial
operation will be maintained, if not improved.

TOWN CLERK
Recording the birth of a child born to U.S. citizens
living in Europe, issuing a new birth certificate
for a child born out of the country and now adopted,
revising a death certificate due to delayed medical
examiner reports
such are the common
"uncommon" responsibilities of the Clerk's office.
Participation in the reenactment of the country's
first Town Meeting in the 1600's, held at the
Kennedy Library, gives one a sense of history which
the Town Clerk is responsible to maintain. The
Clerk is the one person who is required to see that
the Town's records are maintained in proper order
and preserved for future generations.
Much work needs to be done to restore the Town's
records dating back to 1728, when Lunenburg was
THE HERITAGE OF NEW ENGLAND -
incorporated. Besides actual restoration

techniques, an environment is necessary which is
both heat and humidity controlled as well as
fulfilling fire and security concerns. Such is the request to the Massachusetts Council on the

Budget. Proposed action includes cleaning, repairing and rebinding of old records, as well as
-ofilming, and updating the Town Hall vault.

===PAGE 71===
VITAL STATISTICS - 1983
Following are those records of Births, Marriages, and Deaths that either occurred in our Town or records which were forwarded to
this Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents are urged to notify the Town Clerk if in-State records have not been
received and recorded. If a Birth, Marriage, or Death occurred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunenburg Town
Clerk's office if certified records are presented. In some cases the Town Clerk's records are supplemented by records of burials which
appear elsewhere in this report. In order for these records to also be included in the oficial Vital Statistics records, the family of the
deceased should present a certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk. In accordance with recent changes in the General
Laws marriages which occur here but relate to licenses which were obtained elsewhere in the State are no longer included in our official
Town Records.

Annual Town Meeting and Election
April 2, 1983
Special Town Meeting - Fiscal Year 1983 Warrant
Annual Town Meeting - Fiscal Year 1984 Warrant
Moderator, Richard J. Bishop, declared no quorum present, therefor meetings were not held. (Under the Provisions of Chapter 12 of
the Acts of 1983, the Board of Selectmen cancelled the Town Meeting until some future date. Election held as scheduled.)
ARTICLE 1. To choose by ballot the following officers: Selectman for three years; Assessor for three years; Town Clerk for three
years; Treasurer for three years; Tax Collector for three years; Auditor for one year; Constable for three years; two School Committee
Members for three years; Park Commissioner for three years; Commissioner of Trust Funds for three years; Cemetery Commissioner for
three years; two Library Trustees for three years; one Library Trustee for one year; Planning Board Member for five years; Housing
Authority Member for five years; and to vote on the following questions: Question 1. "Shall the Town of Lunenburg be allowed to assess
an additional $93,387.00 in real estate and personal property taxes for the fiscal year beginning July 1, 1983?"; Question 2. "Shall an act
passed by the General Court in the year nineteen hundred and eighty-two, entitled "an Act providing for recall elections in the Town of
Lunenburg" be accepted ?".

Excerpts From Annual Town Meeting Minutes
June 13,1983
Town Clerk Robert J. Ebersole presented former Town Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer Mary L. Padula with a plaque from the
Town in recognition of her years of service to the Town.
David Rodgers, 82 Highland Street requested that the following former Town employees be recognized for their years of service to
the Town totaling over 191 years: Mary L. Padula, Richard J. Eley, James P. Deming, Leona Bertram, Agnes M, Withington, Alfred H.
Gauthier, Helen L. Walker, Miriam W. Cheesman, Robert C. Coleman and Lillian Penan.
SPECIAL FISCAL YEAR 1983 WARRANT
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of $900, or
some other sum or sums, to supplement the amount voted under Article 24 of the 1982 Annual Town Meeting Warrant for the purchase of a
police cruiser, and to rescind that part of Article 24 of the 1982 Town Meeting that directs "one of the present cruisers to be traded" and to
authorize the Selectmen to transfer that cruiser to the control and jurisdiction of the Fire Chief, or act anything thereon. (Approved by
Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
FISCAL YEAR 1984 ANNUAL MEETING WARRANT
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, or act
anything thereon. VOTED.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of the Town
of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on April 3, 1982 to be effective July 1, 1982, and substituting
therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Commitee and appearing in the report of the Finance
Committee, such action to be effective July 1, 1983, or act anything thereon. (Finance Committee recommends approval.) VOTED.
ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of the Town
of Lunenburg" by (a) deleting from the last paragraph of SECTION 19. SICK LEAVE the words "for a maximum of 60 days accumulated
unused sick leave" and inserting in place thereof the words" based on a percentage of his accumulated unused sick leave up to maximum
of sixty (60) days,"; and (b) deleting from SECTION 24. TRAVEL REIMBURSEMENT the words "17¢ per mile" and inserting in place
thereof the words "20¢ a mile" ,or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Board of Selectmen to appoint a Town Accountant in accordance with the
provisions of Chapter 41, Section 55, of the General Laws, as amended, said Accountant to serve in accordance with the provisions of
Chapter 41, Sections 56-61 inclusive of the General Laws, the term of said Town Accountant to commence July 1, 1983, and further to vote
to abolish the office of Town Auditor, or act anything thereon. (Finance Committee recommends approval.) DEFEATED.
ARTICLE 5. To see if the town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, all sums of money
necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for Fiscal Year 1984 and to fix the salaries and compensation of all elected
officers of the Town and any other items included in the Budget of the Finance Committee, or act anything thereon. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY ON MOTION OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE THAT THE BUDGET BE TAKEN UP AT THIS TIME AND THAT THE
TOWN VOTE TO APPROPRIATE THOSE SPECIFIC SUMS OF MONEY RECOMMENDED IN THE LAST COLUMN OF THE BUDGET
APPEARING ON THE WHITE HANDOUT SUPPLIED BY THE FINANCE COMMITTEE WITH THE FOLLOWING EXCEPTIONS: THAT
THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 02, AUDITOR BE CHANGED FROM 0 TO $5,000; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 05, TREASURER
SALARY BE CHANGED FROM $8,500 TO $13,000; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 06, TREASURER ADMINISTRATION BE
CHANGED FROM $52,500 TO $62,500; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM TOWN ACCOUNTANT & ADMINISTRATION BE
CHANGED FROM $25,000 TO 0; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 32, FIRE DEPARTMENT BE CHANGED FROM $93,812 TO $94,812;
THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 61, DRAINAGE EXPENSE BE CHANGED FROM $28,000 TO $16,000; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER
ITEM 64, SCHOOL DEPARTMENT BE CHANGED FROM $3,953,000 TO $3,957,500; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 69,PARK
DEPARTMENT BE CHANGED FROM $37,778 TO $38,378; THAT THE AMOUNT UNDER ITEM 71, MEMORIAL DAY BE CHANGED
FROM $100 TO $125, AND TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION:
$210,000 be transferred from the unrestricted fund balance;
$ 35,000 be transferred from overlay surplus to the Reserve Fund;

Chapter 43 - An Act Establishing Separate Accounts for Non-Resident Students Tuition in Certain Cities and Towns. Chapter 71 of the
General Laws is hereby amended by inserting after section 71E the following section: Section 71F: In any city or town which accepts this
section, all monies received by the school committee as tuition payments for non-resident students and as state reimbursements for
students who are foster care children shall be deposited with the Treasurer of the Town or City and held as separate accounts. The
receipts held in such a separate account may be expended by said school committee without further appropriation for expenses incurred
in providing education for such non-resident students or for such students who are foster care children, notwithstanding the provisions of
section fifty-three of chapter forty-four. A city or town may appropriate funds for expenses incurred in providing education for such
non-resident students or for such students who are foster care children which funds shall be expended by the school committee in
addition to funds provided from other sources. (Finance Committee recommends approval.) VOTED.

ARTICLE 18. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article V of the By-laws of the Town of Lunenburg by adding thereto the
following new section, which shall be Section 13: (a) Any person or body that has lawful control of a public or private way or of improved
or enclosed property used as off-street parking areas for businesses, shopping malls, theaters, auditoriums, sporting or recreational
facilities, cultural centers, residential dwellings, or for any other place where the public has a right of access as invitees or licensees, shall
be required to reserve parking spaces in said off-street parking areas for any vehicle owned and operated by a disabled veteran or
handicapped person whose vehicle bears the distinguishing license plate authorized by section two of chapter ninety, according to the
following formula: If the number of parking spaces in any such area is more than fifteen but not more than twenty-five,one parking space;
more than twenty-five but not more than forty, five per-cent of such spaces but not less than two; more than forty but not more than
one-hundred, four per-cent of such spaces but not less than three; more than one hundred but not more than two hundred, three per-cent
of such spaces but not less than four; more than two hundred but not more than five hundred, two per-cent of such spaces but not less
than six; more than five hundred, but not more than one thousand, one and one-half per-cent of such spaces but not less than ten; more
than one thousand but not more than two thousand, one percent of such spaces but not less than fifteen; more than two thousand but less
than five thousand, three-fourths of one per-cent of such spaces but not less than twenty; and more than five thousand, one-half of one
percent of such spaces but not less than thirty. (b) Parking spaces reserved under the provisions of paragraph (a) shall be identified by the
use of above grade sign with white lettering against blue background and shall bear the words' Handicapped Parking: Special Plate
Required. Unauthorized Vehicles May Be Removed at Owner's Expense"; shall be as near as possible to a building entrance or walkway;
shall be adjacent to curb ramps or other unobstructed methods permitting sidewalk access to a handicapped person; and shall be twelve
feet wide or two eight-foot wide areas with four feet of cross hatch between them. (c) No person shall leave an unauthorized vehicle within
parking spaces designed for use by handicapped person or disabled veterans or in such a manner as to obstruct a curb ramp designed for
use by handicapped persons as a means of egress to a street or public way. (d) The punishment for violations of subparagraphs (a)
through (c) shall be as follows: $15.00 for the first offense; $25.00 for the second offense; and for each subsequent offense, the vehicle
may be removed. VOTED.
