ARTICLE 29. To see if the Town will vote to petition the Great and General Court of Massachusetts (in the event of a "yes" vote on
ballot question #2) to amend Chapter 67 of the Acts of 1982 -- "An Act providing for Recall Elections in the Town of Lunenburg". VOTED
TO PETITION THE GREAT AND GENERAL COURT OF MASSACHUSETTS TO AMEND CHAPTER 67 OF THE ACTS OF 1982 "AN
ACT PROVIDING FOR RE-CALL ELECTIONS IN THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG", AS FOLLOWS:
SECTION 1 After the word "provided" add, "except, the maximum number of members of multimember board that may be recalled
is a simple majority."
SECTION 2 1st paragraph, in the last sentence delete the words "twenty per cent" and substitute "twenty-five per cent"
SECTION 2, 2nd paragraph, delete the word "forthwith" and insert in place thereof "within five working days";
SECTION 3, 1st sentence, delete the words "without delay" and insert in place thereof the words "within five working days";
SECTION 3, 1st sentence delete the word "forthwith" and insert in place thereof "within five working days";
SECTION 3, 1st sentence, delete the words "not less than twenty-five nor more than thirty-five days" and insert in place thereof"not
less than thirty nor more than forty days";
SECTION 3, 1st sentence, delete the words "sixty days" and insert in place thereof "ninety days";"
SECTION 3, 2nd sentence, delete the words "sixty days" and insert in place thereof "ninety days";
SECTION 8, Delete the words "two years" and insert in place thereof "one year"
77
===PAGE 80===
If approved by the Great and General Court, the Act, as amended, would read as follows: SECTION 1. Any holder of an elected
office in the Town of Lunenburg may be recalled therefrom by the registered voters of the town as herein provided, except the maximum
number of members of a multimember board that may be recalled is a simple majority.
SECTION 2. Any one hundred registered voters of the town may initiate a recall petition by filing with the town clerk an affidavit
containing the name of the officer sought to be recalled and a statement of the grounds for recall. The town clerk shall thereupon deliver
to said voters making the affidavit copies of petition blanks demanding such recall, copies of which printed forms he shall keep
available. such blanks shall be issued by the town clerk, with his signature and official seal attached thereto. They shall be dated, shall be
addressed to the selectmen and shall contain the names of all the persons to whom they are issued, the name of the person whose recall is
sought, the grounds of recall as stated in the affidavit, and shall demand the election of a successor in the said office. A copy of the petition
shall be entered in a record book to be kept in the office of the town clerk. Said recall petition shall be returned and filed with the town clerk
within twenty days after the filing of the affidavit, and shall have been signed by at least twenty-five per cent of the registered voters of the
town, who shall add to their signatures the street and number, if any, of their residences.
The town clerk, shall within twenty-four hours of receipt submit the petition to the registrars of voters in the town, and the registrars
shall within five working days certify thereon the number of signatures which are names of registered voters of the town.
SECTION 3. If the petition shall be found and certified by the town clerk to be sufficient he shall submit the same with his certificate
to the selectmen within five working days, and the selectmen shall within five working days give written notice of the receipt of the
certificate to the officer sought to be recalled and shall, if the officer does not resign within five days thereafter, order an election to be held
on a date fixed by them not less than sixty nor more than ninety days' after the date of the town clerk's certificate that a sufficient petition
has been filed: provided however that if any other town election is to occur within ninety days after the date of the certificate the selectmen
shall postpone the holding of the recall election to the date of such other election. No person shall be subject to recall if his term of office
expires within ninety days of the certificate. If a vacancy occurs in said office after a recall election has been ordered, the election shall
nevertheless proceed as provided in this section.
SECTION 4. Any officer sought to be removed may be a candidate to succeed himself, and unless he requests otherwise in writing,
the town clerk shall place his name on the ballot without nomination. The nomination of other candidates, the publication of the warrant
for the removal election, and the conduct of the same, shall all be in accordance with the provisions of law relating to elections, unless
otherwise provided in this act.
SECTION 5. The incumbent shall continue to perform the duties of his office until the recall election. If then re-elected, he shall
continue in office for the remainder of his unexpired term subject to recall as before, except as provided in this section. If not re-elected in
the recall election, he shall be deemed removed upon the qualification of his successor, who shall hold office during the unexpired term. If
the successor fails to qualify within five days after receiving notification of his election, the incumbent shall thereupon be deemed
removed and the office vacant.
SECTION 6. Ballots used in a recall election shall submit the following propositions in the order indicated:
For the recall of (name of officer).
Against the recall of (name of officer)
Immediately at the right of each proposition there shall be a square in which the voter, by making a cross mark (x) may vote for either of the
said proposition. Under the propositions shall appear the word "Candidates", the directions to the voters required by section forty-two of
Chapter fifty-four of the General Laws, and beneath this the names of candidates nominated in accordance with the provisions of law
relating to elections. If two-thirds of the votes cast upon the question of recall is in the affirmative, the candidate receiving the highest
number of votes shall be declared elected. If more than one-third of the votes on the question are in the negative, the ballots for candidates
need not be counted.
SECTION 7. No recall petition shall be filed against an officer within ninety days after he takes office, nor, in the case of an officer
subjected to a recall election and not recalled thereby, until at least ninety days after the election at whilch his recall was submitted to the
voters of the town.
SECTION 8. No person who has been recalled from an office or who has resigned from office while recall proceedings were
pending against him, shall be appointed to any town office within one year after such recall or such resignation.
SECTION 9. This act shall be submitted for acceptance to the voters of the Town of Lunenburg at the next annual town election or a
special town election, whichever occurs first, in the form of the following question which shall be placed on the official ballot at such
election- "Shall an act passed by the General Court in the year nineten hundred and eighty-two, entitled "An Act providing for recall
elections in the Town of Lunenburg' be accepted?" If a majority of the votes cast in answer to said question is in the affirmative, this act
shall take effect, but not otherwise. "Wording changed by Act of Legislature, Chapter 506 of the Acts of 1983. Approved by the Governor
November 18, 1983.

YOUR SCHOOLS
BROOKS HOUSE: (Houses Office of School Superintendent)
The "Brooks House" and property was originally deeded to the Town of Lunenburg on December 3, 1937 by Ida
and Mary Brooks. In September 1946, when the school department was experiencing an overcrowded condition at the
elementary level, the school committee, "after careful deliberation, planned to solve the problem by seeking the
use of the so-called Brooks residence. The selectmen cooperated in granting permission for this house to be
taken over by the school department." (from the 1946 Town Report)
In checking the Town Report for. 1893, it is noted that Lunenburg was part of a four-town school union com-
prising the communities of Ashby, Lunenburg, Townsend, and Winchendon. In 1905, Winchendon withdrew, and Union
#24 remained a three-town union until July 1, 1954, when Lunenburg withdrew and had a full-time superintendent
of schools.
Superintendent's who have served from 1893 to the present are:
David B. Locke (12 years) 1893-1905; Fairfield Whitney (3 years) 1905-1908; E. L. Haynes (4 years) 1908-
1912; Herman C. Knight (8 years) 1912-1920; John Bacon (6 years) 1920-1926; William H. Millington (6 years)
1926-1932; Wesley E. Nims (9 years) 1932-1941; Charles M. Robinson (8 years) 1941-1949; Maurice P. Billings
(13 years) 1949-1962; Ralph W. Brown (8 years) 1962-1970; William R. Holland, Ed. D. (8 years) 1970-1978;
and William C. Allard, Ed. D., 1978-
LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL:
This building was built in 1925 at a total cost of $54,000., including building, land, and equipment, to
house grades seven through twelve. In 1957, when the present high school was built, the building was
refurbished to contain grade four and two sections of grade five and was known as the Elementary Annex. From
1957 to 1968, of grade four and either one, two, or three sections of grade five were housed in the building.
During 1968-69, when double sessions were in existence, grade three attended in the AM session and grade four in
the PM session. From 1969 to 1978, grade three was located in this school. In 1978, the name was changed to
Lunenburg Primary School and housed kindergarten and grade one pupils and effective in the 1983-84 school year,
our pre-school program as well.
PASSIOS ELEMENTARY SCHOOL: (Currently houses grades 2-5)
"During the early years of world war II the growing problem of adequate school housing facilities became
such that construction of a new school in the near future was inevitable. Money was appropriated in 1946 for
problem. obtaining working plans and specifications at which time it was thought an eight-room building would relieve the
"Soon after, various parcels of land totalling some forty acres were acquired for construction of future
municipal buildings and for recreational purposes. The rapid growth of the town immediately following the war
changed the future outlook to the extent that the building committee, authorized by vote at the annual meeting
total of twenty-four to be required within the next few years.
in 1949, realized that no less than sixteen additional elementary classrooms were necessary immediately, with a
"After extensive investigation of relative costs for various designs and types of construction, and
committee inspections of more than thirty recently completed schools throughout New England, the layout and
school teaching with a minimum of maintenance costs over a period of years.
design of this building was chosen as one which would provide safe and adequate functional facilities for grade-
"Plans and specifications were completed for a sixteen-room building designed so as to allow for the future
addition of eight rooms, and a contract was awarded for this construction in July, 1951.
"In September 1952, the Town authorized construction of the additional eight rooms following which bids
were received and a contract was awarded to the contractor who built the original building (S. W. Haynes &
Associates of Fitchburg).
"Funds totalling $1,062,000. were provided by the Town at various times from 1949 to 1952 by appropriations
and transfers, and by borrowing within and beyond the normal debt limit, as authorized by General Laws, by
Special Acts of the Legislature, and the Emergency Finance Board. Both projects have been approved by the
Massachusetts School Building Assistance Commission and are eligible for partial reimbursement." (from the 1953
Town Report)
December 6.
The 24-room elementary school was opened on September 21, 1953 with dedication exercises held on Sunday,
83
===PAGE 86===
In 1957, an additional twelve-room addition was added to the building and in September 1975, two special
education resource rooms were added.
Official naming of the Lunenburg Elementary School as the "Thomas C. Passios Elementary School" wa.
announced on Sunday, November 21, 1966 to an overflow crowd of townspeople and educators assembled in the
Elementary School auditorium to a public testimonial to Mr. Passios. The announcement, made by Howard F. Brock,
Chairman of the Lunenburg School Committee, was made "in recognition of the contributions made by Mr. Thomas C.
Passios in thirteen years of outstanding service to the Town of Lunenburg as principal of the Elementary School."
Principals of this school have been Thomas Passios (1953-1966); Richard Dustin (1966-1974), Paul Ralston
(1974-1981); and Concetta A. Verge (1981- ).
TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL: (Currently houses grades 6-8)
"The new Turkey Hill Middle School is scheduled to open for classes on February 24, 1969. Initially, stu-
dents and teachers will occupy only the classroom wing while remaining work on the gym, cafetorium, and kitchen
is being completed. The complete building should be ready for occupancy by April 1, 1969. (Architect: Stoner
Associates at a total cost of $1,310,608.)
"Despite many construction delays caused mainly by strikes and inclement weather, the Building Committee
and the school administration are extremely pleased with the new school. As we become able to actually see the
language laboratory, the science laboratory, the beautiful library, the convertible classroom and many other
features, we felt assured that the Town will indeed be proud of the new addition to the Lunenburg School System
designed to assure that our children obtain the type of education so vitally needed in our world today." (from
the 1968 Town Report)
Principals of this school have been Floyd Barney (1968-1981) and Carol A. Eaton (1981- ).
LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL: (Currently houses grades 9-12)
"On the basis of a complete and thorough set of educational specifications drawn up by Dr. Leo T. Doherty
of Worcester, in cooperation with members of the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School Building Committee, the new
building was planned to provide classroom space for a maximum of 650 pupils. The gymnasium and auditorium leach
of which accommodates 600), the cafeteria which will take care of 300 at a sitting, and the other central facil-
ities such as homemaking and industrial arts departments, were considered adequate to meet the necessary demands
of a junior-senior high school population up to 1000 pupils. In other words, to accommodate a student enrollment
exceeding 650 pupils, regular classroom space must be added.
"The first bonds were issued on December 1, 1955. Later bonds totalling $110,000., were sold as the money
became needed. The terms of all the bonds is fifteen years.
"The building was ready for occupancy in 1957 at a total cost of $1,216,992.95. The town has acquired a
new school which is not only extremely functional but attractive as well. Absence of frills and full use of
available space make this building one of the most economical in Massachusetts and another tribute to its Archi-
tect, Mr. Harry J. Korslund." (from the 1958 Town Report)
In 1963, twelve classrooms were added to the building.
In review, high- school principals from 1900 to the present were: Fred H. Hadlock (1900-1904); George
W.
Emerson, Jr. (1904-1909) Edwin R. Bemis (1909-1912); Claude A. Gray (1912-1915); Joseph A. Harwood (1915-1922);
J. Clifton Williams (1922-1923); Channing H. Greene (1923-1924); Charles Lamb (1924-1925); Miss Fannie E. Woods
(1925-1934); Herbert D. Tinker (1934-1940); Andrew J. Karkos (1940-1957); Harold R. Alley (1957-1961); Richard
J. Carey (1961-1981); and A. Ray Peterson, Ed. D. (1981- ).

No-School Signals - 1983-84
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with the Highway
Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen emergency; there-
fore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise their personal judgment as to
the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of No-School Signals for 1983-84 will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION INDICATES "NO SCHOOL" AT THE
FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED -
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5, including Pre-School
10:00 AM
PM Session of Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of radio stations WEIM and WFGL in Fitchburg; WLMS
in Leominster; as well as WSRS, WTAG and WFTQ in Worcester; and WBZ, WHDH and WROR in Boston, as promptly as the
decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls as rapidly
as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus contractor might not be
notified in time. Parents can help by not allowing their children to make unnecessary calls. The cooperation
and understanding by all will be very much appreciated.

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my sixth annual report
as Superintendent of Schools in Lunenburg. This annual
report covers the year ending December 31, 1983.
As you read the reports of the other administrators,
you will receive a more detailed accounting of the events
that have taken place in the schools. The purpose of
this report is to summarize the total aspect of the Lun-
enburg Public Schools.
COMMITMENT TO EXCELLENCE:
I feel strongly that we have entered a new era of
commitment to excellence for our public schools. In Lun-
enburg,we have long had a tradition of hard work, strong
curriculum, and high expectations from our students and
staff. These efforts have risen to a new high in Lunen-
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
burg. We are very thankful to our students who on a
Superintendent of Schools
daily basis, prepare their lessons and participate in
school activities. We need to show our staff the appre-
ciation that we have for the daily monitoring of the
students' work and the high standards of evaluation that they impose upon their students. The responsibility
that each of us carries every day is enormous. We must accomplish so much in such a short time that the pressure
sometimes becomes overwhelming. The parents of our students continue to be very supportive of our efforts. We
realize that we have very demanding policies in attendance, grading, homework and discipline. None of these
policies are enforced to harm students' educational goals, but they are enforced by all of us to help students
attain responsibility and commitment. The results of these policies usually means a well prepared, responsible
citizen entering our society. We will constantly strive to provide our students with the best possible atmos-
phere and staff conducive to the acquisition of knowledge. We thank the citizens of Lunenburg for providing the
necessary resources to accomplish this.
JANUARY 1983 - JUNE 1983:
We had a very successful last half of the school year in terms of student accomplishments and our ability to
meet their needs. We had a budget which allowed us to properly staff our schools and at the same time provide
our staff and students with the equipment and material necessary to provide a quality educational system. During
the year we were able to adjust many of the losses we had experienced during the preceding years. This was
accomplished by the town providing a budget with an 8.4% increase over the previous year. With this budget we
made tremendous strides in our programs and in providing the material necessary to accomplish our goals.
In May, 1983 the town voted the schools an increase of 4.3%. This increase was not sufficient to maintain
our staff and expenses at the previous levels. Rather than cut teachers and increase our class sizes, we made
the decision to cut expenses. Consequently, we cut our expenses over the previous year's expenditures by seventy
thousand plus dollars. This cut has severely curtailed our ability to properly fund our expenses this year.
SEPTEMBER 1983 - DECEMBER 1983:
Along with our losses in the expense portion of our budget, we also found ourselves with an increased
enrollment. For the first time in many, many years, we have more students this year than we had the year before.
We are not completely sure why or how this happened, but this placed added burdens on our financial situation.
In our attempt to realize sensible and proper class sizes, we cut the expense portion of our budget to find the
$180,000. we had to cut from our original request. In doing this we left many of our accounts with not the
proper amount to adequately assure ourselves that we would be able to accomplish all that needed to be done.
We must continue to give credit to the large number of people who continue to support our athletic programs.
This community continues to provide great financial resources both to the interscholastic program at the high
school and to the other sports programs in the community organized by the Park Department, Little League, Babe
Ruth League, Pop Warner League, etc. The young people of this community share in the contribution made by these
organizations with their participation and enthusiasm. We all enjoy the process when we see the benefits result-
ing from young people enjoying themselves in worthwhile endeavors.
We are very fortunate to have an atmosphere of support for our schools that we receive from our staff,
parents, students and administrators on a daily basis. Many of the problems that are faced by other school
systems are removed from us by us possessing quality educators, enlightened parental involvement, and informed
administration. In addition to these factors we are blessed with students who love, believe in, and commit them-
selves to receiving a quality educational experience. We attempt to demonstrate the results of all of the
individuals involved by productions in drama, music, art festivals, sports and academic competition. We have
88
===PAGE 91===
over the years brought much recognition to Lunenburg and the Lunenburg schools by these endeavors. Yet what
remains is recognition of the daily work that is done by all the staff. This work needs to be understood by
the community! I applaud the efforts of these daily tasks which may go unrecognized by many.
THE FUTURE:
The future for the Lunenburg Public Schools remains critical only in terms of financing. I feel strongly
that we have demonstrated our commitment as a school system to providing this community with a quality school
system. We have demonstrated this by our support to retain quality educational professionals, by introducing
new programs that meet the needs of our ever increasing capabilities of our students, by our sensitivity of
monitoring our budgets to reflect staff adjustments and reorganization of our school structure and by demanding
from all who are employed no less than the best. We are however at the limit. We must now approach the issue
of how do we finance the schools in the future. We have tightened up the budget in every area possible; we are
demanding and receiving from staff every available ounce of energy they have to give. We cannot expect more
"fat" to be trimmed. There is no more that can be done. If more is to be cut then the citizens must accept
less as a product. If this happens, then it also happens in the areas of discipline, expectations, results and
more importantly, in pride.
We must look to a new method of financing the schools. What has happened under Proposition 21 in the
community is that the schools have steadfastly received less of the percentage of the tax base each year. This
is shown by the fact that in FY-80 we received a 77.6% of the tax dollar to support schools. In FY-84, we
received 60.0% of the tax dollar for schools. In four short years we have lost almost 18% of the tax dollars
that used to be allotted to schools. This means that we have needed to tighten up with the hope that we would
not lose too much. We are however at that point. Consequently, we need to decide how to make funds available
for a quality school system.
I submit that we must as a community need to look at school funding in the following manner:
1. At least 65% of the tax increase allowed by Proposition 2½ be applied to schools.
2. All of the money appropriated as Chapter 70 (school aid) monies be available to schools.
3. All school transportation monies returned to the community be made available to the schools.
It may be conceivable that when these three sources of revenues are combined, there may be more money
available for schools than necessary. However, I will indicate that I would never submit a budget for schools
that was more than what was necessary. I do feel however that the increase in state aid has been increased in
order to support the losses in school financing under Proposition 21. In any event, schools in Lunenburg should
not be financed by a formula of "what is left" to give to the schools, but rather what monies are needed to
continue or return to a quality school system.
Last year, I established some goals that we would attempt to accomplish this school year. Let me take
these one at a time and respond to them.
1. TO CONTINUE TO SUPPORT THE ACHIEVEMENT MONITORING SYSTEM PRESENTLY STARTED AT THE ELEMENTARY SCHOOL AND
EXPAND THE CONCEPT THROUGHOUT ALL GRADES.
In the elementary school we have continued this program and expanded it into the language arts and
mechanical usage area. We are very pleased by the efforts of the administration and staff at the
elementary school to continue their efforts to provide documentation of the students' efforts and needs.
In the middle and high schools we have increased our usage of standardized tests in academic areas so as
to be able to match our results against national and regional norms. It is important to note that we
are well aware that testing should accomplish two goals
1. establish results against acceptable norms
2. identify weakness in your academic program.
2. MAINTAIN A REALISTIC BUDGET PRESENTATION TO THE TOWN.
I feel strongly that we have always presented to this town a realistic budget for the operation of
the school department. There have been groups or individuals that might have felt that we asked for too
much or not enough. On the other hand, we must ask for what is needed in line with what the town
expects out of the school system and realistically what it can afford.
3. MAINTAIN OPEN COMMUNICATIONS WITH ALL TOWN DEPARTMENTS AS TO NEEDS, GOALS AND JOINT EFFORTS.
This goal has been accomplished simply by the process of implementing Proposition 2½. The
restraints on the availability of finances forces all of us to be more in tune with each other's needs
and attempts to provide service. We will continue to encourage these interactions and improve the pro-
cess by which they take place.
89
===PAGE 92===
4. PROVIDE THE ADMINISTRATORS OF OUR SCHOOLS WITH THE SUPPORT AND ENTHUSIASM THEY SO JUSTLY DESERVE.
We, as a school system, and this community, have demonstrated this support in many ways. As a
community, you have shown your support for their intelligence, abilities and their concern for the stu-
dents under their care.
5. INCREASE PUBLIC AWARENESS OF WHAT IS TAKING PLACE IN OUR SCHOOLS ON A DAILY BASIS.
We have addressed this goal as a school system by newsletters, media information and public talks
with parents. The school committee has established this goal as a high priority for them. A community
relations committee has been formed and it is attempting to decide its direction and its responsibility
toward meeting this goal.
CONCLUSION:
In conclusion, I must pay special tribute to our school committee. This town has been most fortunate in
selecting highly qualified, very interested and extremely conscientious members. They spend hours of their
personal time reviewing documents and information made available to them in order to arrive at intelligent and
proper decisions. They are individuals who provide us with tremendous accessability so as to have them hear the
concerns, questions, and criticism of a very complex and important department in this town. We applaud them as
individuals and respect them highly as a committee. To my fellow administrators, 1 personally thank them for
their daily effort to carry out policy and creating an environment conducive to learning. I wish to salute an
outstanding teaching staff. They demonstrate by the results of the product high ability to transpose knowledge
and the ideals necessary to produce an educated and responsible citizen. To the students, I pay special tribute
for being the reason all of us are here everyday. They show great enthusiasm for their studies and contribute
to our daily environment by their happiness, unpredicability, love and their commitment. Special thanks go to
"Ginny" Welch who serves this school system and town with high quality performance and love. To Bob Okerman for
his concern and help to me on a daily basis. To the secretaries, cafeteria workers, and custodians
your
love for our children is exemplified on a daily basis and we and they thank you for it. Finally, to the
citizens
thank you for your respect and concern.

PRIMARY AND ELEMENTARY SCHOOL ENROLLMENT:
Enrollment at the elementary level appears to have finally stabilized. In September of 1983 we registered
one hundred ten kindergarten children (entry class). Our exiting class of fifth graders numbers one hundred
thirteen. This appears to indicate that the decline of enrollment that we have felt at our level has ended. We
are looking forward to a consistent, stable enrollment. As a matter of fact, we may even be facing an increase
in the overall number of students attending the Primary and Elementary schools.
Below you will find listed by grade level the combined enrollment of the Primary and Elementary schools as
of February 1984.

PROGRAMS AND ACTIVITIES AT THE PRIMARY AND ELEMENTARY SCHOOLS:
Mrs. Maki holds the attention 06 her pre-schoolers.
Pre-schoolers enjoy a break in

As previously mentioned there were some staff cuts at the Primary and Elementary Schools this year. How-

most of our programs.
LUNENBURG PRE-SCHOOL:
The Lunenburg Pre-School Program, which was formerly a SPECIAL NEEDS program, has been expanded this year.
Originally the pre-school served only three, four, and five year olds who were determined to be in need of man-
dated special education services.
Through a grant written by Mrs. Janet Maki, funds were secured to develop an integrated pre-school program
which includes a number of "regular education" tuition students and some "special education" students. The pro-
gram is known as LEIP (Lunenburg Early Integration Program). We currently boast a pre-school population of
fourteen children.
The major goal of this program is to ready all children involved for the "regular education" kindergarten
at the Primary School. The pre-school focuses its attention on socialization and readiness skills. There is a
heavy infusion of language development throughout all readiness and socialization activities. Special services
are provided through the special needs department for those children who require such assistance. All children,
however, in addition to the regular curriculum, are treated to art, arts and crafts, physical education, music,
and cooking activities.
As a result of the reorganization at the Elementary School, Mrs. Janet Maki became the pre-school teacher
this year. Mrs. Vicki Whittenkraus serves as a grant-funded Language Communications Specialist for the pre-
schoolers.
Pre-schoolers attend a class five mornings a week. The program has been a great success. It is our hope
to be able to accept additional tuition students in future years and to expand this program.

TEAMING FOR READING AT THE FIRST GRADE:
Reading instruction at the first grade level has been re-structured. All children are now grouped homo-
geneously for reading and move from homeroom teacher to reading teacher for approximately two hours (reading
block) a day.
The purpose of this ability grouping is to insure a more individualized approach for instruction. It also
allows a child to move comfortably at his/her own pace. Homogeneous grouping prevents the "pushing" of a child
who is not ready to move on and also guarantees that a child who is ready to move at a faster pace may do SO.
Instruction is geared to the specific level of each group. Materials are carefully matched to each group's
needs. The first grade teachers constantly monitor the reading achievement of their students and plan their
instruction accordingly.
The children have adjusted very well to this new structure and appear to enjoy the opportunity of having
exposure to more than one teacher in the first grade.

skills enjoyed by a first grader.
WRITING AND MECHANICS/USAGE:
A great deal of attention has been paid to the area of written expression and the grammatical skills
required to achieve in that area.
Classroom teachers and the building administration met and determined goals and objectives for the students
in grades K-5 in the areas of Mechanics/Usage and Compositional/Writing skills.
After a considerable amount of discussion and work, a system for monitoring student achievement in the area
of mechanics/usage was developed. It is known as AMP-M/U (Achievement Monitoring Program in Mechanics/Usage).
As with its counterpart AMP-Math (developed last year), AMP-M/U is based on the teaching and monitoring of
specific grammatical skills determined to be most pertinent at each grade level. Children in grades 1-5 will be
93
===PAGE 96===
tested approximately four times each year to ascertain achievement delineated skills. Individual student print-
outs, which graphically display skills each child has mastered or must work on, and class summary sheets which
facilitate planning for small group and individual instruction, are generated after each test administration.
Written Expression is the second component of the total Writing and M/U program. Specific writing skills
also have been determined for each grade level, beginning with pre-writing skills (objectives) of the fifth
grade. Teachers form their instruction in writing on the agreed upon objectives.
The children are asked to write a minimum of three times a week after instruction on certain skills has
taken place. A rating system has been developed and incorporated in the correction of compositions, reports,
etc. (rating of writing form). An emphasis has been placed on the importance of writing and on the need to
express oneself through the written word (not only creatively but in the expository sense as well). Classroom
teachers have driven this message home to the children and all parties appear to be vigorously working toward
this end.

CHAPTER I - READING AND MATH:
The Chapter I Reading and Math Program is a
federally-funded program which provides additional
educational help to children who are experiencing
difficulty in the basic skill areas of reading and
math.
Through funds provided by this grant a group of
tutors who have special training in reading and math
have been hired to provide direct service to children
who are not functioning on grade level. The Chapter I
specialists meet with small groups of children and
provide them with tutorial assistance in their
specific areas of weakness. Although the Chapter 1
tutors use different methods and materials in their
instruction, the regular school curriculum is
followed. In other words, the AMP-Math objectives
which are followed in the regular classroom are also
followed in the Chapter I Program. The same holds
true of reading objectives. The benefits of the

Recently, Chapter I purchased additional software for the two Apple Ile microcomputers owned by the pro-

Street Writer which enables students to write their own stories on the micro; Word Blaster, a vocabulary rein-
forcer; Clock, which provides practice in telling time and other math software which insures drill and practice
in math facts.
Use of microcomputers is only one component of a multi-faceted remedial reading and math program. Numerous
teacher-made and commercial materials help insure the success and achievement of the Chapter I participants.

MICROCOMPUTERS AT THE ELEMENTARY SCHOOL OR THE ADVENT OF THE APPLE:
The microcomputer and its unlimited potential has found its way into education. It is not uncommon for|
school systems today to have microcomputer laboratories or microcomputers in their classrooms.
Lunenburg, having realized the positive impact this equipment might have on its students, has expended a
great deal of time and effort to study and plan for the proper course and use of the micro in its schools.

Mrs. Jane Murray, a staff member who had previously been trained in the use(s) of the microcomputer, repre-
sented the Elementary School at educational microcomputer workshops and conferences where she expanded her know-
ledge and expertise and has emerged as a well-versed, avant garde, resource person in the area of microcomputers
in education. Mrs. Murray in turn has transmitted her enthusiasm and expertise to fellow-staff in the Lunenburg

schools by conducting a twelve week in-service program "Microcomputers in the Classroom" in which fifty-six
The problem the Lunenburg Elementary School faced was that it had numerous well-trained teachers ready and
willing to utilize the microcomputer as an educational tool within their classrooms, and only four microcomputers
(previously donated by the P.T.O. and/or purchased with grant funds).
A group of interested and supportive community members became aware of this particular need in the schools
and conducted a well-organized, highly successful drive to raise funds for additional microcomputers. As a
result of their efforts this group which has now become known as the Lunenburg Endoument Committee secured
thirteen Apple II microcomputers and two printers for the Primary, Elementary, and Middle schools.
The Primary and Elementary schools received seven of these micros and have put them into immediate use.
Two of the micros will be located at the Primary School and will move from classroom to classroom as needed.
The Elementary will retain the remaining five micros and utilize that equipment in classrooms and with larger
groups in a laboratory setting. All equipment will remain portable, however, insuring maximum use.
EDUCATIONAL IMPLICATIONS:
The microcomputer at the elementary level is being used as an educational tool which is integrated into all
aspects of the regular curriculum. It is perceived as a unique instrument which will encourage the development
of logic, problem solving, and higher cognitive skills as well as a vehicle to enhance the existing curriculum.
The microcomputer is also used to reinforce and refine learned skills as well as to provide simulated exper-
iences for the children.
A kindergarten through grade five computer curriculum has been developed incorporating activities designed
to familiarize children with the micro, expose them to social studies and science simulations, teach them key
board and word processing skills, and introduce them to various levels of LOGO language.
Computer is not a separate or additional subject to be taught at this level. It is, instead, an integral
component of the total educational program at the Elementary School.
IN-SERVICE FOR CLASSROOM TEACHERS:
The ever changing, always demanding role of the teacher necessitates a constant need for the professional
to be kept current with trends in education. There are various means by which professional educators may remain
up to date with educational innovations and the new technology.
95
===PAGE 98===
Reading the educational journals, attending conferences and workshops, and enrollment in graduate courses
offered at colleges and universities are ways many of our teachers keep up with the most effective programs and
best methods of dealing with students. However, it is incumbent upon the school system to offer training in
educational methodologies and programs if the teachers are expected to be involved with or implement them. This
is known as teacher in-service.

Through various grant funds secured by staff in the school system (Chapter I Block Grant, Pre-School, and

In-service is to veteran teachers as Geritol is to tired blood. We are all enjoying renewed vigor and
expertise in our work thanks to these types of programs.
CHILD STUDY PROCESS:
A process by which individual students and stu-
dent needs might be carefully examined and discussed
has been developed at the Elementary School by Mrs.
Lois Davenport, elementary level guidance counselor.
The name of the process is the Child Study. The
objective behind this process is to give any adult
(teacher, parent, administrator or specialist), who
has a concern about a child's ability to cope with
the regular school program, an opportunity to express
that concern and have access to individuals who might
shed light on or present alternatives which would
allow that child to succeed in school.
Child Study meetings are scheduled through Mrs.
Davenport who also serves as chairperson for each of
these meetings. Notices are sent out to all staff
inviting them to attend Child Study meetings on any
Mrs. Davenport chairs a Child Study on a fourth grade
child they feel they might provide insight on or have
student. (Left to right: Mrs. Verge, Mrs. Drwin,
reason to express concern about. In attendance at
Mrs. McCue, Mrs. Davenport and Mrs. Vignale.)
these meetings usually are classroom teachers,
parents, specialists (special education) and building
administration. A profile of the child and his or
her issues is normally given. Student records are reviewed. Discussion takes place and recommendations for
specific interventions or program modifications are made at that time. In some cases, students suspected of
having specific learning problems are referred to special education for testing and further evaluation. In other
cases, the recommendations of the Child Study team are immediately implemented and monitored closely to ascertain
the effectiveness of the interventions. A time line is established for evaluating the outcome of the recommended
intervention. If required, further action would later be taken.
The Child Study format is the perfect vehicle by which a prompt response is made to teachers concerns and
student needs.

Report of Middle School Principal
INTRODUCTION:
Middle School staff and students welcomed Jeff
O'Neill to a new role this year--that of Administrative
Assistant. Mr. O'Neill has been a sixth grade teacher
at the middle school for eleven years prior to this
appointment. It has been our pleasure to work together
to provide support for our teachers who deliver educa-
tional opportunities to your children, Janet Melendy
joined the middle school staff this year as our new
secretary. She has provided enormous support for us
throughout the school.
All middle school staff members have continued to
strive for creative, yet not costly ways to supplement
and enrich the curricular program as well as the student
activities areas. This has required additional fund-
raising and increased planning by teachers. These
efforts have paid off for our students in many ways dur-
ing the course of the year.
The middle school's enrollment has remained constant

been able to retain its strong staff and rigorous
curricular program including the core subjects as well
as the special subjects. Our students are expected to
meet high academic standards in math, English, science,
social studies, reading and foreign language. We are
most fortunate in times of fiscal restraint to maintain

In physical education, a climbing wall was added to our gymnasium as part of the PROJECT ADVENTURE program.
Our health education program is a separate subject for each student and stresses drug and alcohol education at

Mr. Grenier explains a point.
Hardworking students.
Our eighth grade students receive additional challenges in the areas of French, Spanish, Latin and algebra
for high school credit if they opt to enroll and if they complete the requirements to our satisfaction. The
other option in language are Conversational Spanish and French as well as Life Skills in language. All but
eight students in the present eighth grade class take a foreign language. Last summer, Mrs. Kathy McGuigan
along with Mrs. Carolyn Simsarian, took eighteen Latin Club members to the National Classical League Convention
at the University of Rochester in New York state for a week. We have very enthusiastic Latin students. They
celebrate the Ides of March and numerous other Roman festivals. Our French students, under Mrs. Jo Meloon's
direction, are currently planning a weekend trip in May to Quebec City in Canada. Spanish students took trips
to a Mexican restaurant and to a Spanish musical production at the University of Massachusetts at Amherst. Stu-
dents must raise all of their own money for these trips. Both Mrs. McGuigan and Mrs. Meloon administer nation-
ally standardized examinations in their respective languages. Our language program stresses strong in-school
experiences as well as outside-of-school experiences.
We strongly emphasize excellence in all subjects, but especially in the area of basic skills. Each April
our eighth grade students take the Minimal Competency Exams in reading, writing, mathematics and listening. Our
students do very well, nearly 96% meet minimal standards in all four areas, but we insist year-round on standards
much higher than minimal competency. The eighth graders also take the Differential Aptitude Test which assists
guidance counselor, Tony Falcone, and eighth grade teachers in planning high school courses withour students.
This exam helps staff identify strengths which can be capitalized on in high school courses. This year's seventh
grade students did especially well on the Stanford Achievement Test. All one hundred forty-two seventh graders

During the year, middle school teachers have also
been involved in preparing themselves for the integra-

areas. From November to February, middle school
teachers studied with elementary school teachers under
the direction of Mrs. Jane Murray on the subject of
applying microcomputers to their own subjects. Thanks
to strong community support through the Lunenburg
School Endowment Fund and the efforts of the Superin-

our total to eight. Teachers have begun to develop a
curriculum for the microcomputer at each grade level.
This planning for microcomputer use is at the develop-
mental stage. Our hope is to plan for inclusion of the
microcomputer in different subjects at various grade
levels in a very careful and well thought out way, so
that our scope and sequence of goals for microcomputer
use brings the best educational opportunities for our

PARTICIPATION AND INVOLVEMENT OF STUDENTS:
Since the development of student leadership at
this age level is a high priority for the staff, we
have provided many opportunities for students to
participate and involve themselves in planning for the
school. Jeff O'Neill and Tony Falcone began the year
by holding elections in each homeroom for student
council members. The council consists of eighteen
representatives and meets approximately three times per
month to discuss school issues and plan activities.
Our students seek greater input in the planning for the
school and we have responded. They have planned and
held three very successful dances with refreshments,
dance contests and prizes. Each Wednesday morning,
with the strong support of Tony Falcone, the council
sponsors the sale of donuts, juice and milk before
school. The council has sponsored two roller skating
parties and one ice skating party with fine participa-
Students really do dance!
tion from the student body. All proceeds from these
council activities went to open a school store. Pro-
ceeds from the school store will go to benefit the stu-
dents in the form of activities at the end of the year, future field trips and even beautification of the school.
Jeff O'Neill and Tony Falcone have done an outstanding job working with our council members to provide involve-
ment and to build leadership.
Many students, parents and teachers were involved in the middle school Christmas Fair again this year.
Local merchants, parents, staff members and interested community members contributed goods, services and their
time to the Fair. The fair was open two nights to the public, at parent conferences, and three days for the
students, including the fifth graders. Jeff O'Neill continued in his role as coordinator of the Christmas Fair,
and was pleased to donate $900. to the Lunenburg Community Council. The entire school was proud to donate this
money to the community because SO many times the community has supported the school.
Three seventh grade teachers (Harris Karis, Tom Starr and Philip Chalifoux) once again, organized our annual
magazine drive which provided $6800. to the middle school students for after-school activities, field trips,
magazines for the library, assemblies, and audio-visual equipment. Over 60% of our students participate in this
annual fundraiser. Our students assist the three staff members in the overall organization of this drive by
collecting from homerooms and by keeping the records of the sales. We thank the community for their support of
this fund-raiser for it directly benefits our students.
This year, in order to make our magazine sale funds go further, we did have to charge a small tuition for
our after-school activity program. We conducted two sessions, which were organized by Dick Bishop and Bob
Saudelli, sixth grade teachers. Programs included were woodworking, basketball workshop, crafts in home
economics, art projects, downhill and cross-country skiing, backpacking, musical production, computer club,
Latin club, school newspaper and yearbook. In Lunenburg, we believe that an after-school program is necessary
for students of middle school age. In the future we hope to broaden our program of offerings for students.
101
===PAGE 104===
Our students participated in numerous field trips
at each grade level this year. Each one was carefully
planned to reinforce classwork and curricular areas of
study. Our magazine drive funds assisted with the
trips, and students contributed a share each time.
Our students visited the Lowell National Historic Site
which features the manufacturing of textiles, the
Museum of Science, the Worcester Art Museum, Mechanics
Hall in Worcester, The Freedom Trail Exhibits in
Boston, George's Island in Boston Harbor, and the
General Motors assembly plant in Framingham. Students
have benefited greatly from out-of-school experiences.
Janet Melendy, our new secretary, instituted a
training program for seventh and eighth grade students
to work in the office and learn how to respond to
visitors, telephone calls and to handle the intercom.
This has been very productive in building personal
responsibility among these students. It has also
helped us in the office greatly, since Mrs. Melendy is
the sole secretary for the entire school of four hun-
dred fifty students.
GUIDANCE:
A key component in a middle school is the guidance
area. At Turkey Hill, Miriam Light serves sixth and
seventh graders as counselor and Tony Falcone serves
our eighth graders, as well as teaching three sections
of math. Both individuals meet students in groups and
alone. They work to help students improve relation-
Some students dress uniquely.
ships with other students, their parents and teachers.
Students depend on both for help in organizing
academic work, planning for the future, and in making responsible choices. Both counselors meet with parents
very often to provide the necessary liaison with school and teachers. Adolescents and preadolescents have many
developmental problems which need time and emotional energy to solve.
Miriam Light coordinates the testing of seventh graders in the fall of the year, administering the Stan-
ford Achievement Test to all students. She then follows up with all teachers and students regarding the results.
Students receive a copy of their results, along with the interpretation. Teachers use this information to more
thoroughly diagnose student needs. Eighth grade students are given the Differential Aptitude Test to assist in
the assessment of strengths and weaknesses. The results are shared with all teachers and students; they
frequently help in the determination of course selection for the high school. Miriam Light and Sandralee Turner,
(grade seven teacher) oversee the Basic Skills Testing which occurs in April. All grade eight students are
given Minimal Competency Exams in reading, writing, math and listening. We notify parents and students immedi-
ately if there is a failure. Students do receive intensive help at the high school and do have the opportunity
to retake the exam.
Tony Falcone plans and coordinates the transition
of our eighth graders to the high school. All eighth
graders visit Monty Tech and the high school in Lunen-
burg. Mr. Falcone works with each student to decide
with him/her what courses in ninth grade would offer
the greatest challenge. In the seventh grade, Mrs.
Light conducts career exploration classes to give the
students a basic picture of what is waiting for them
in the future. Mrs. Light works closely with grade
five teachers to establish a strong orientation pro-
gram for fifth graders. Students visit the middle
school for most of the day and visit a cross-section
of classes.

SPECIAL NEEDS DEPARTMENT:
The middle school provides excellent special needs
services to its students. Dr. Linda Howard is the
system coordinator of special needs and her office is
located at the middle school. Marybarbara Hughes is
the chairperson for the middle school and is assisted
by Sue Petrowicz, Gerrie Stachow and Steve Kyajohnian.

is our school adjustment counselor. Each staff person
in special needs delivers direct services to students,
in addition to conducting testing necessary for evalua-
Kathy McGuigan and Tony Falcone.
tion.
The middle school in Lunenburg is a beautiful facility and it is maintained by Larry Paton head custodian,
Ray Sideleau and Paul Bellafiore. Each of these individuals take great pride in keeping the building like new.
Each summer a different section of the building receives new paint or special repair work. The middle school
is constantly used by outside groups whenever the students and staff are not present. Our custodians manage to
keep the building in great shape in spite of its frequent use. Our students and staff are most fortunate to
have the atmosphere which exists at the middle school.
Our cafeteria staff is headed by Mrs. Anita LeCuyer who strives to provide the best service and most
nutritional food possible. One highlight of the day for middle schoolers is lunch. Over half of our student
population buys lunch at the middle school. Nearly every student partakes of items from the alacarte section.
Delores Brown and Lynne Thomas represent the nursing staff for the entire school system. They serve the
middle school for a portion of the day and are on call everyday in case of emergency. They conduct all of the
tests necessary for state statute in addition to seeing individual students. They also work very closely with
the health teachers to provide information and resources for classes.
As was indicated earlier in this report, Janet Melendy is the only secretary at the middle school. All
clerical work is done by her in addition to all the student, parent and staff demands placed on her daily. Her
lunch and coffee breaks are provided for by having teachers cover the office on a rotating schedule. Students
have been trained to work and cover the office as well. Mrs. Melendy has done a fine job this year in every
area. Her attitude is positive and welcoming to students, staff and parents.
Individual help from Ms. Petrowicz.

She was greatly assisted last summer by Agnes Withington who had served as middle school secretary until

fine sense of humor. She guarded our material resources and protected our finances. Agnes could account for
every nickel in our coffee fund! She was a dedicated and tireless worker and friend. We could always depend on
her, and we hope she'll continue to visit us and think of us.
In CONCLUSION, we ask for your continued support for the future. We need you to support our programs
because we want your children to have the educational opportunities that are absolutely necessary in our society.
We are not pressing for luxuries or frills. We are pushing for adequate materials and staffing which keep us on
target with today's educational needs.

PHILIP M. CROUSE

Administrative Coordinator
Administrative Coordinator
Over the last several years, the high school has made important and substantial changes in its curriculum.
The purpose of these changes has been to improve the quality of instruction and the level of student academic
performance.
104
===PAGE 107===
Two guidelines have been followed. The first has been to insure that all students receive a sound basic
education. Secondly, the curriculum has been reviewed to insure its flexibility: courses must both challenge
students to the limit of their intellectual capabilities, and at the same time encourage them to explore the
full range of academic opportunities at the high school.
To achieve the first objective, students at the grade 9 level are guided to enroll in mostly basic academic
courses, to insure that they have fundamental academic and intellectual skills. Secondly, courses have been
added in the various departments with emphasis upon instruction in basic skills in all areas. Thirdly, the
flexibility of the curriculum has been improved by the addition of a number of courses which students may take
for a semester (half-year). The use of semester courses encourages students to explore areas of the curriculum
in which they might not be able to schedule themselves for a full year. This will allow (in future years) the
establishment of distribution guidelines under which students will explore all areas of the comprehensive
curriculum.
There are a number of new course developments in the various departments which the high school is pleased
to implement as part of its overall academic objectives.
ENGLISH AND WRITING PROGRAM:
The English Department has increased its emphasis on writing skills in order to improve the writing of all
students. Intensive English classes of small groups have been established at each grade level to allow more
individual instruction for students needing special help in reading and writing. Across all grades and levels
of ability, emphasis is increased upon frequent expository writing assignments and documentation of student
progress in composition.
A Business English course has been established with the cooperation of the Business and English Depart-
ments. The purpose of this course is to allow students to blend their interest in writing for business purposes
with the reading and interpretation of literature taught by a member of the English faculty. This cooperative
effort has been most successful in focusing student's attention on the needs for improved writing for business
situations, as well as the appreciation of literature as a personal satisfaction for all students.
SOCIAL STUDIES:
A number of changes have been made in Social Studies to insure basic academic skills, and to increase
student exploration of the social sciences. The basic grade nine course, World Cultures, was revised by the
department in the summer of 1983, including a new textbook. The revision of the course retains the study of
some ancient history, but also provides new studies of areas of the world including developing and third world
nations. This will provide a better global perspective for students.
Mrs. Jean Koykka and the 1982-83
Mr. Dennis Keeney and student prepare for
Lunenburg High School Math Team.
rocket launching in Energy and Aeronautics.
A new Civics course has been added to the curriculum: Citizen's Rights and Responsibilities. This course,
which is required for graduation, includes a review of the Constitution and the Bill of Rights, and passage of
required tests on the Constitution for graduation.
The department has also expanded its offerings with a course in Twentieth Century United States History.
The course in Psychology has been opened to sophomores and students of all levels of ability, resulting in a
fifty percent increase in enrollment. A Human Relations course has also been added to the social studies
105
===PAGE 108===
BUSINESS DEPARTMENT:
The Business Department has added a school store, operated just off the school's main lobby, as an adjunct
to the curriculum in General Business.
A Business Mathematics course has also been added to increase skill levels in intermediate mathematics as
an adjunct to studies in other business courses.
Mr. Fred Crellin and Jackie Dennihan review Economics.
The 1982-83 Championship Chess Team.
INDUSTRIAL ARTS:
In Industrial Arts, addition of several semester courses has allowed more flexibility for all students to
choose courses such as: Consumer Car Care, Home Maintenance, and Photography. Results have included an improved
integration of students enrolling with different levels of academic ability, as well as continued erosion of sex
stereotyping.
HUMAN DEVELOPMENT:
In addition to courses offered in Health and
Human Maintenance, the course Thresholds to Adulthood
has been added to allow students to explore issues of
marriage, parenting, old age, and adolescent/adult
relationships.
PHYSICAL EDUCATION:
In the Physical Education syllabus, a new emphasis
has been placed upon assessing and improving physical
fitness. Students are now tested in physical fitness
at the beginning and end of the year, and placed in
corrective physical education (extra) periods when
necessary to improve basic fitness.
CURRICULUM SUPPORT ACTIVITIES:
Many curriculum support activities take place in
the high school throughout the year. The following are
three specific examples of important programs initiated
by the faculty.
The Social Studies Department has, over the last
two years, organized a series of assembly forums for
elected officials. The students have had an opportunity
to hear and to ask questions of candidates for the
positions of State Representative and State Senator;
the Town Treasurer/Town Clerk has also spoken to such a
Barry Ollikkala, Valedictorian of Lunenburg
forum. Recently, the Congressman for this congressional

For three years, the Art Department has maintained a chapter of the National Art Honor Society. This
national honorary society provides a recognition and activities for students with high achievement in the Visual
Arts Program at the high school. Several members of the society have been honored in each of the last three
years for their work in local, regional, and statewide art competition.
Mrs. Sheila Tetler, the high school art teacher,
also initiated and received funding for a grant in
school year 1982-83 which provided professional
development training for six teachers of social
studies, health, home economics, and foreign
language. These workshops, conducted in cooperation
with the Worcester Art Museum, assisted faculty
members in developing curriculum and materials for
the teaching of the fine arts as part of their
respective other disciplines.
STUDENT SUPPORT GROUP:
In the fall of 1983, students of Lunenburg High
School organized a local chapter of the state-wide
group known as Students Against Driving Drunk (SADD).
This organization seeks the cooperative efforts of
students to prevent drunk driving. One hundred and
fifty students in the school signed the statement
of agreement to provide alternatives in real situa-
tions where the possibility exists of students
driving under the influence of alcohol. A total of
fifty students are actively engaged in the leader-
ship activities of the organization.
Mrs. Sheila Tetler in visual arts class.
PARENT-COMMUNITY SUPPORT GROUP:
Also in the fall of 1983, under the leadership of
health teacher Barbara Jezak, and school nurse Delores
Brown, a large group of parents and members of the
community founded the Lunenburg Community Action Net-
work (L-CAN). This organization has as its objective
the prevention of drug and alcohol abuse in the
community, which includes the high school. More than
four hundred members of the Lunenburg community con-
vened for the initial meeting on this effort in the
fall. After being guided in its initial deliberations
by a distinguished panel of Lunenburg citizens, and
under the very able chairmanship of Mr. Fritz Foerster,
the organization has continued meetings and dis-
cussions aimed at designing action programs, to pro-
vide alternatives in prevention of alcohol and drug
abuse in the community.
ADMINISTRATIVE REORGANIZATION AT THE HIGH SCHOOL:
Over the last two years an administrative reorgan-
ization has been put into effect at the high school.
The objective of the reorganization has been to pro-
vide better support services for teaching and learn-
ing, which is the main business of the school.
Within the faculty, the decline in enrollment and
the budgetary restrictions of Proposition 21 required
the elimination of department heads supervising each
subject. To partly restore the necessary coordination
and support for teaching, Program Coordinators have
been put in place over the several departments com-
prising distinctive teaching areas. Three program
coordinators are now in place, coordinating and facil-
Miss Jane Whitney working in the 1982-83
itating work over Applied Sciences (Business, Home
faculty interdisciplinary fine arts workshop.
Economics, and Industrial Arts), Humanities (English,
107
===PAGE 110===
Foreign Language, and Social Studies), and Research
Sciences (Mathematics and Science). These program
coordinators have reduced teaching loads and
responsibilities to facilitate work such as budget
preparation and curriculum improvement. Cooperative
teaching across traditional department lines is an
additional objective of the reorganization.
In the administrative office, Administrative
Coordinators with part-time teaching assignments
have replaced a single full-time assistant princi-
pal.
The administrative coordinators have
distinctive duties in assisting the principal; for
example, student activities is one assignment;
master scheduling may be one assignment of the
other administrative coordinator.
Coordinator positions in program and admin-
istration are designed to be rotating assignments
(on a three-year basis) to provide career mobility
for staff.
Mr. Don Piermarini and Lunenburg High School
Advanced Biology class study Atlantic marine life.
Also in the office, new assignments have increased efficiency in the schools. The position of Registrar
was established, to combine the duties of the former guidance secretary with general student records responsi-
bilities in the main office. An Attendance Clerk position has also been established to provide full-time
recordkeeping and monitoring of attendance. These assignments, under the general supervision of the Principal
Secretary/Treasurer, have greatly facilitated the work of the school.

- RETIREMENTS DURING 1983 -
MIRIAM W. CHEESMAN
May 2, 1966 - June 30, 1983
Miriam Cheesman began as school librarian during the year
1965-66. She had served as assistant librarian at the Ritter
Memorial Library (Lunenburg) for a period of five years and had
also worked at public libraries in Arlington, Mass. and Fort
Wayne, Indiana. She received her B. A. degree from Swarthmore
College in Pennsylvania and her B. S. in Library Science from
Simmons School of Library Science in Boston. She also served
with the United States Navy in Communications from 1943 - 1950
and at the time of her discharge on October 26, had a commission
of Lieutenant Junior Grade.
In her last year of service with the Lunenburg Public
Schools, she served as the single librarian in the school system.
The quality of her work with students and staff during her tenure
were certainly recognized by everybody. We wish her retirement
years to be filled with memories of her past experiences and the
Miriam W. Cheesman
excitement of new adventures in the future.
ROBERT C. COLEMAN
May 10, 1976 - June 27, 1983
Bob Coleman began his employment with us under the CETA program when he was hired for the position of
utility man in the Lunenburg Public Schools by the Board of Selectmen. On May 9, 1977, when his employment
under the CETA program terminated, the school department hired him as a custodian at the high school. On
April 30, 1979, he transferred to the elementary school as senior custodian until April 27, 1981 when he
returned to the high school as senior custodian. On August 27, 1981, he transferred once again--this time to
the primary school to serve in a dual role as primary school custodian and town hall custodian. It was from
this position on June 27, 1983 that Bob Coleman retired after seven years of service. We wish him many happy
years of retirement.
SHIRLIE W. RUGGLES
May 4, 1966 - March 2, 1984
Shirlie Ruggles was hired as a part-time clerk/typist in the elementary school on May 4, 1966 and the
following September assumed the duties as secretary for the Title III, ESEA program. She held this position
until July 1, 1968 when she was elected as secretary to the elementary principal. This position she held until
her retirement on March 2, 1984 after eighteen years of outstanding service as a member of the secretarial staff
of the Lunenburg Public Schools. (See also the Report of the Elementary School Principal)
We all appreciate Shirlie's efforts on behalf of the students of this school system and wish her only the
best in her retirement years ahead.
HELEN L. WALKER
August 25, 1969 - July 15, 1983
Helen Walker began her employment with the Lunenburg Public Schools
by her appointment as payroll clerk in the Central Office. She assumed
and carried out this responsibility until she applied for the position
of guidance secretary in the high school in 1973. She transferred to
the high school on August 27, 1973. In 1981, due to a reorganization of
the main office at the high school, Helen was appointed as Registrar.
Helen retired on July 15, 1983 after serving the school system with
distinction and outstanding merit for a period of fourteen years. We
all miss her but wish her many happy retirement years.
Helen L. Walker
113
===PAGE 116===
AGNES M. WITHINGTON
May 31, 1962 July 26, 1983
Agnes Withington began her tenure with the Lunenburg
Public Schools as a part-time clerk in the Office of School
Superintendent. By March 4, 1963, the work load at the
Superintendent's Office necessitated her employment to be
increased to full-time. On March 6, 1967, she assumed the
responsibilities of Central Office bookkeeper and payroll
clerk. In 1968, when the new middle school was completed,
she was appointed as principal's secretary, and transferred
to that school as secretary to Mr. Floyd F. Barney. Agnes
retired from this position on July 26, 1983, completing
twenty-one years of "outstanding commitment and skill" to the
Lunenburg Public Schools. (See also report of Middle School
Principal)
When Agnes was interviewed by a middle school student in
January 1982, she indicated that she loved to travel having
visited such exotic places as Bermuda, Scotland, England,
Puerto Rico, and the Canary Islands. Now that she has more
Agnes M. withington
time for herself we hope she will be able to add to that
list. All of us miss her and wish her Godspeed in her
retirement years.
